the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. president. - the debate is closed. the transport sector is one of the few sectors in the eu that is experiencing rapidly increasing emissions. to achieve the goal of limiting climate change, greenhouse gas emissions must be reduced substantially, and therefore, of course, emissions from road transport are also crucial. the commission's 2020 target proposal had two different targets: one for passenger cars and one for light commercial vehicles. my original proposal in committee was that we should have the same level of ambition regardless of the type of vehicle - it would probably have been more efficient. it had also confirmed the level of ambition that many had already set themselves. the socialist ambition was a more ambitious agreement, but we accepted the outcome because the passenger cars were being negotiated at the same time from a slightly better starting point. we did not foresee that the german government of the day would block the agreement on cars, which is why cars have been postponed and not put to the vote along with light commercial vehicles. in view of the climate package we voted on in the environment and industry committees last week, it is important that the commission now live up to the long-term target for light trucks as they have committed to, namely to reach the target for light commercial vehicles with emission limits between 105 and 120 grams of co2 per kilometre by 2025. it will be crucial if we are to achieve our climate ambitions. i am therefore disappointed with the result, i endured it and i hope that the commission will take up the work that must now be done.
for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. i have worked and drafted laws with holger krahmer many times in this parliament, but this time we have not been able to agree - we thought it was too different this time. we had a fantastic opportunity this time to vote for rules that save money, improve the economy, increase our export opportunities and benefit european businesses and the environment at the same time. the commission's own evaluations showed that an ambitious level had been achieved in all these objectives at the same time. even if we had set the limit of 110 grams already in 2020, it would have been achievable and there the benefits of fuel savings would have been much higher than the costs of the new light trucks. the fuel savings we had obtained for the most ambitious of the commission's examined proposals would have been equivalent to just over two euro cents or 20 swedish øre per kilometre. why should all the craftsmen of europe pay more unnecessarily? for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. my starting point in the debate tonight is that we must find a smooth and somewhat understandable way of dealing with the question of better regulation. one of the main sources of criticism of the eu institutions has been the difficulty of understanding who is responsible for what and who to hold accountable when dissatisfied with the outcome of legislation. the lisbon treaty brought many simplifications, but we have sometimes tended to introduce new complexities into the system that make it difficult for an outsider outside the eu bubble to keep up. i think one such area is the subsidiarity test of national parliaments. the eu will legislate in the area where there is clear european added value and the member states where there is no such added value. it is therefore obvious that we have the tools to safeguard the principle of subsidiarity, which is very good. i think, however, that we should ask ourselves whether the yellow card model of national parliaments in its present form is really effective. there are no common standards governing the action of the parliaments of the member states when they issue these cards. the short response time may also mean that some parliaments do not have time to deal with matters that they are initially sceptical about, while others take the uncertain for granted and raise this yellow warning card without having made a full or reliable assessment. the current system is far from ideal, but it is clear to me that the work of change is under way and must be based on the system being simple, understandable, predictable and transparent for the citizens, and this should in no way restrict the involvement of the european parliament in this decision-making process. i also think that national parliaments must be able to object to proposals when their main points do not respect the principle of subsidiarity, but cooperation between this parliament and the national parliaments to achieve the common guidelines would be very desirable. i see a great need for it. to strengthen the quality of legislative work, we must also ensure that there are reasonable resources for quality assurance in all institutions. in times of economic hardship, it is easy to make populist demands for savings. we must not underestimate that there are additional costs of inadequate legislation that we also have to deal with, because badly designed legislation can be avoided if only adequate resources are available, which is needed in libraries, in committee offices, etc. i thank you for your time. i would urge everyone to vote in favour of this report.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am very concerned about the refit initiative. it can be seen that some parts are good in themselves. it is important to work towards clarifying and simplifying eu legislation. however, the initiative has been a disaster in terms of the working environment and the conditions of workers in general. in that part, it could be harmful. the work on health and safety at work in europe has been weakened for a number of years. we see many reports today of worsening working conditions. to continue to weaken would be the wrong course to take. it is not just that everyone must have the right to a healthy and safe working environment. good working environments are also a win-win for everyone - for employees, for employers and for the economy. i therefore believe that the commission should immediately reassess this part of the initiative. we in europe must do more, not less, for the working environment.

, member of the commission. - mr president, i would like to thank the members for the work they have done on this directive, which i believe is a cornerstone of the digital internal market. i am convinced that it will contribute to the dynamism of this market by facilitating the entry of small suppliers and innovative services, as your rapporteur marielle gallo has just said. this text will also contribute to a wider distribution and a better choice of online music offerings in europe. i am therefore very grateful to each and every one of you, in particular to marielle gallo for the quality of her work and to the fictitious rapporteurs, and to the whole committee on legal affairs and its chairman, mr lehne, for having brought the work forward with a strong political will to reach a conclusion on this matter in due course, that is to say, at the time when the mandate of your parliament is about to end. collective management societies play a key role in the management of copyright and related rights. they grant licences for the online distribution of music. the dynamism and potential of this sector are beyond dispute. there are more than 70 authors' societies in the european union and one million authors are members of these societies. it has been found that online music sales in europe have increased from 200 million euros in 2004 to 1.2 billion euros in 2012. however, we also know that despite this, some collective management societies have had difficulty adapting to the constraints of rights management for online or cross-border exploitation. in the past, we have even seen some situations which are quite abnormal or unacceptable in view of the role played by collective management in the economy of the creation and dissemination of culture. i do not want to go into specific cases of lack of transparency or inappropriate investment. it is clear to all that work had to be done to improve the management of the operation of these companies at european level. we've made it a priority. i am convinced that this directive will provide the impetus for the necessary modernisation of these collective management companies, firstly in terms of their governance and transparency. the new provisions will give the beneficiaries a right to control management and will require the identification of the beneficiaries and the distribution of the sums collected within a maximum period of time. the directive brings about a real modernisation with more dynamism in the relations of companies with service providers. it lays down operating conditions which these companies will have to fulfil in order to be able to grant multi-territorial licences. finally, the directive gives these companies the right to be represented by other companies if they cannot meet these criteria themselves, which will reduce the number of licences needed to launch online services in europe. i think that the adoption of this directive at first reading, if you will, demonstrates your commitment to consolidating the role of collective management in the digital single market. i thank you in advance for the confidence you have shown and will continue to show in this new stage. we will then have to ensure that this text is implemented by the member states within a rapidly agreed timeframe, but also that it is appropriated not only by management companies but also by rightholders and users. this dossier, ladies and gentlemen, shows in this important, major and sensitive area that dialogue is possible on copyright, despite some difficult debates in the past and sometimes very antagonistic positions. it also shows that balanced solutions and dynamic compromises supported by a very large majority are possible. i should like to take this opportunity, mr president, to say a few words about the continuation of our work in the field of copyright at commission level. after the adoption of this directive on the management of collective societies, the directive on orphan works and the memorandum of understanding on end-of-life works, after our commitments in the framework of the 'licences for europe' dialogue to facilitate access to works throughout europe and by the greatest number, we have not finished our work on copyright. as we announced in our communication of 18 december 2012 on content in the digital single market, we are currently conducting a thorough review of the copyright acquis. we are doing this on the basis of serious legal and economic studies, and a wide public consultation which was launched in december. it is not surprising that this consultation has aroused and is arousing very, very great interest on the part of all those involved in the culture of citizens, of the people entitled to rights, including you in parliament. that is why, in agreement with my colleagues neelie kroes and androulla vassiliou, we decided, because this consultation aroused great interest and because we had set it too short a time, to extend the consultation period until 5 march next, so to give an extra month. i had heard this wish from many of you and from many people in the cultural and creative fields. we will use these contributions to feed into the impact assessment work and discussions on a possible revision of the eu copyright rules. as you will have understood, we proceed step by step, allowing the time necessary for consultation and dialogue, in a serious manner, identifying solutions based on facts and real problems where and when they exist. that is also why we have proposed to mr barroso, president of the commission, that this work should be continued, after consultation, by the publication of a white paper before the summer, probably in june, which will lay the foundations for a future adaptation of the copyright legislative framework, so that copyright lives up to its time, that of the single market and that of the internet. the white paper will aim to put forward a series of guidelines and concrete proposals for debate on the basis of an impact analysis. in fact, this will be a second stage in this exchange and consultation with all the stakeholders whom we, on the commission side, with neelie kroes and androulla vassiliou in particular, consider essential to making progress on this matter. this will enable your assembly, the day after its renewal, and the next commission, to take up this subject in turn, in order to carry out this task on a solid and transparent basis. in the same way, i would like to say a few words of clarification on another matter on which you have asked me in the past weeks about the procedures for notification and action. this is a topic that, while not strictly related to copyright, is very important for trust in the digital economy. it is clear that we must ensure that illegal content, not lawful content, is removed from the internet through clear, transparent and balanced procedures, in full respect of the application of fundamental rights. some of you have told me that you want a legislative initiative on this point to bring more guarantees and harmonization at european level and others have told me that you do not want such an initiative. it is therefore a sensitive subject, linked to fundamental rights, on which there are very divided opinions, as the consultations we have held show. i think that any initiative on this subject, whether legislative or not, must involve parliament fully. i do not think it is reasonable to do so at this stage of the legislature. we will not have time to do things in a serious, objective and transparent way. that is why i did not wish to put such an initiative before the college at this time. we'll have a chance to talk about it and work on it again. i wanted to say this, after having discussed it with many of you, thanking you for your understanding on this point.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank commissioner barnier and his whole team at the commission for this extraordinary work on the proposal. i would also like to thank marielle gallo in particular for the outstanding work she has done in this area. i also thank all my colleagues for the very, very good cooperation during the process. as we know, copyright has long been a highly controversial subject, but despite this we have managed to reach agreement on this directive across all party lines. collective management of copyright is one of the cornerstones of a well-functioning modern copyright system. in today's complex reality, it is virtually impossible for a rightholder to manage his rights himself. it is therefore crucial that the companies responsible for collective rights management are transparent and operate in the best way for their members. we are now putting in place important rules to guarantee the rights of individual composers and artists vis-à-vis the companies. the money collected must now be paid out as soon as possible, which is very important. the individual artist or composer should also be able to compare different european companies in order to demand improvements of his own or move to another, more well-managed company if he or she is not completely satisfied. the directive will greatly facilitate the creation of digital music services throughout the eu, giving us music lovers a wider choice of music services. for composers and songwriters, it means the possibility of their music being spread throughout the eu and reaching all 510 million citizens who live here. i am convinced that it will be beneficial for cultural diversity that many of our citizens will have easy access to music from all corners of europe. with europe's outstanding creativity and diversity, there is great potential. today we're putting an important piece of the puzzle in place.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, marielle gallo. i think she's done a very, very good job on this case. it is true, as our colleague mr wikström said, that the issue of copyright is often controversial in this parliament, which i am very pleased about, because i think it is an important issue that should be controversial and that we need to discuss. we have been very much in agreement on this particular issue, across party lines. the collecting societies are an important part of the economy of the cultural sector. they handle billions of dollars a year. unfortunately, there have been major problems in many collecting societies in many different member states. on the one hand, it is pure crime - there are a number of cases where money belonging to artists and creators has been misappropriated, and on the other hand, there are cases where it has not necessarily been about crime, but about the lack of transparency as to what money is coming in, where it is paid out, why, etc. that transparency has very often been very bad in many countries, in many societies. our hope with the directive - which i think we all share in the political groups - is that it will help to remedy this. so there will be stricter requirements for transparency as regards money and how it is distributed, how much cannot be distributed and what happens to that money. there will also be transparency in the case of the collecting societies' own costs, i.e. salaries of the directors, etc. and this is something that i think a lot of creators are going to be very interested in. i fully agree with commissioner barnier when he says that this directive is good in itself, it is a step, but it is important that creators and artists represented by the collecting societies take up this and make sure that it is pushed forward. it is also important that member states ensure that these laws are enforced, so that they are not just paragraphs on a piece of paper. the transparency we achieve is, i think, the most important, but there are other improvements as well. we're clearly stating that anyone who wants to has the right to use creative commons licenses and other non-commercial licenses, which is good, because some collecting societies have put obstacles in the way of that. in conclusion, i see this as a positive step along the way. i would like to thank the rapporteur again for the excellent cooperation and i would like to thank the commission for what was a really good proposal when it came. now i think parliament has done even better.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am very positive that we are now getting this legislation in place. there is an obvious need for an increasingly tough crackdown on fraud in the financial markets. the solution we have now reached is, in my view, the right one. the new system of criminal sanctions is, of course, about fighting fraud. there is strong evidence that the libor scandal was only the tip of the iceberg and that abuse is relatively widespread in several submarkets. it needs a big cleanup. abuse of power is simply not okay. however, the new system is at least as much about creating efficient, functioning markets where all players compete on equal terms. with clear rules of the game and a strong system of controls to enforce them, fraudsters are prevented from gaining an advantage at the expense of honest operators. this is a win-win situation for everyone. fair markets become efficient markets. effective markets are in turn good for the whole social economy. as the new regulatory framework is now being launched, it is important that we closely monitor the practical effects. i think it will work, but we need to make sure that it does. this is a crucial issue that we must not lose sight of.

rapporteur. - mr president, i will do this in english because it will be a lengthy and complicated debate. first of all, i would like to thank everybody who has been working on this file with me; it has been a momentous task. we have been reviewing the most controversial part of european trade policy: the trade defence instruments, which normally come upon us as anti-dumping tariffs. this is something that is moving higher and higher up the political agenda as we have more high-profile cases. the last high profile case was probably the 47% tariff on chinese solar panels, which i know has led to a lot of debate throughout the european union. we have done it in record speed; it has taken us six months to do something that normally would have taken more than a year. i must say – and i think i speak for all colleagues – that this has been pretty frustrating. we are not happy with the fact that the commission then chose to present such an important and big task to the parliament this late in the mandate, forcing us to work under such pressure. that has made it harder to make good legislation. we also have concerns with the dubious guidelines, about which we had lengthy debates with the commission. this is due to the fact that they were presented as one package. the guidelines and the regulation were presented as one balance. the guidelines have been living their own lives and changing. there has also been discussion over whether they should be adopted before or after the adoption of the regulation. despite that and despite the time pressure, the committee has managed to find good compromises in most areas. we have a total of twelve compromises, stretching from transparency to european parliament scrutiny of trade defence instruments. i just want to mention some of those compromises. firstly on transparency: we have introduced provisions for all interested parties to receive more information about the investigation. that is something that will be appreciated by all parties. we will also try to establish an sme help desk in the area of trade defence instruments that would help both importers and producers affected by trade defence instruments. we have also tried, as i said, to increase parliament’s ability to monitor the implementation of this regulation. we want an annual report from the commission on how they execute and implement this regulation. there are two things that the commission presented as ‘new’ elements of the trade defence instruments that we debated at length. the first of these was a reimbursement of tariffs that were collected during expiry reviews. that is something we decided to remove because, honestly, it involved more bureaucracy than it would have brought added value. the second thing that the commission introduced was an obligation to cooperate for companies under trade defence proceedings, that is taking place ex officio in the commission. that is something that we debated at length, but we concluded that it should not be an obligation, but merely a request from the commission. this is because it might be very burdensome to be forced to hand in all the material that is needed, especially for small and medium-sized enterprises. with these compromises, we will have a substantial improvement of the instruments. it is worth saying that it is worthwhile to vote and to achieve these compromises, even if that is all we get, because they constitute a substantial review of the reform. there are two points, though, where we did not reach compromise, and those are the most controversial parts of the most controversial legislation: the lesser duty rule and the shipping clause. a clear majority of the committee did not want a shipping clause: a provision that would give two weeks advance notice to european companies before tariffs were introduced – tariffs that actually might affect them very dramatically. the lesser duty rule is a rule that makes sure that tariffs will correct the injury done to eu industry, but not go beyond that. a big majority of the committee wanted to remove that lesser duty rule. that is a mistake; going beyond injury would not only lead to higher tariffs and the penalising of trading partners but, more importantly, would increase the price of trade. higher tariffs would not only make imports more expensive, they would also make exports more expensive. i could go on at length to discuss the two controversial topics that we have discussed. last, but not least: we will have a result in the vote tomorrow. this is not the end of this process, as we need the council here. we need someone to cooperate with. therefore, i am very happy that we have the council present and that its representatives have chosen to stay for part of this debate. we need to know whether you are ready to cooperate with us, and whether you are ready to discuss and have an agreement.
member of the commission. - mr president, let me briefly react to what has been discussed here. first of all, mr fjellner made the comment – to a certain extent a complaint – that we came forward with this proposal so close to the elections. i would like to tell you that we have been working on this proposal for quite some time, because if you want to launch such a proposal you first have to start with a proposal and you need a public consultation, then you need an impact assessment, you need interservice consultation, and so on and so forth. it takes quite a lot of time before you get to the college. the house will remember that under the previous legislature there was also an attempt to fundamentally change the tdi rules and that procedure collapsed. therefore, before we introduced the new proposal we tried to find some common ground. it has nothing to do with disrespect for parliament and, on the other hand, mr fjellner, we have to work until the end of the mandate. on the proposals that have been made: as regards amendment 24 on the own-initiative procedures, i have no problem in making an exception for small-sized and micro-sized union producers but i do not want to replace the word ‘obliged’ by ‘requested’. either it is an obligation or it is not. i think it should be an obligation. however, i can live with the exemption for smes. as regards the shipping clause, i share the view that daniel caspary put forward here in plenary and i ask the council – and we will certainly cooperate with you, mr president of the council – to find a solution for the shipping clause. this is not a matter of ideology, it is about whether we can find a solution for a practical problem in a way that ensures that freight already under way does not fall under the anti-dumping duties. on the other hand, we must also be aware of the limits to this, so please try to find together with us a solution to that. on the lesser duty rule, either you have a lesser duty rule or you do not. i cannot foresee going further than the exception that i have put forward, which is the one on minerals. for minerals there is a good reason. it is very important for europe that we have free access to minerals at market prices. that is of crucial importance for our industry. we see more and more often in the markets that countries are putting export taxes on minerals. that is the reason, as ms andrikienė mentioned, that we make this exception for minerals, but i do not see a good reason for the rest. if you go as far – as you now seem to do in the committee on international trade (inta) – as to add many other possibilities for excluding the lesser duty rule, you will simply have to skip the lesser duty rule entirely. in my humble opinion, what is now on the table does not make much sense. i would also like to answer a specific question that was put by mrs saïfi regarding the guidelines. we consider the guidelines a useful tool for stakeholders. they are based on past cases and, in a way, they cement the commission’s best practice. they have undergone public consultation and inter-service consultation and have been revised in line with the comments received. revised versions are now with you and with the council. i have also undertaken to discuss the guidelines with inta myself. dg trade looks forward to receiving comments, if any, from parliament and the council within the next few weeks. thereafter, the guidelines will again be re-examined in light of the comments received and will be subject to a second inter-service consultation. as you can see, the process is fully transparent and inclusive. it will take some more time and i do not expect adoption before april. what i do not – and cannot – support is that they will become part and parcel of the regulation itself. that would make them very inflexible and that cannot be the purpose of guidelines that are based on past cases. they inevitably need to have the possibility of evolving over time. i would like to thank everybody, and above all mr fjellner, for the quick report. the fact that we are not too late does not mean that he has not been very quick. that goes without saying, and i hope that we can keep up this pace and come to a final solution before the end of the mandate.
rapporteur. - mr president, before i turn to the points raised by the commissioner i would like to respond to mr wałęsa, who was talking about whether or not we should vote on the report as a whole. i would like to make clear that the intention to vote on the report as a whole is definitely not an attempt to be dishonest and derail the whole process. it is merely normal procedure. we do it 95% of the time, particularly at the end of the mandate, if we want to guarantee that it survives the end of the mandate. but let us discuss it and see if we can find a solution. the lesser duty rule is essentially what we are debating here. the commissioner is very clear in saying that it is not reasonable to remove more of the lesser duty rule than what is specifically presented by commission. but honestly, in a sense i would say that the commission kind of asked for it. either you believe that the lesser duty rule is an important principle and part of eu trade policy and trade defence or you do not. everybody will find another part that they think is important. you have heard – and you can see in the amendments and the conclusion of the committee – that there are a lot of other concerns besides the commission’s relevant concern about raw materials. you get what you ask for. this is what parliament is concerned about. as you know, i do not personally agree with that position but, tomorrow, we have to take a vote and make a decision. do we want to increase anti-dumping tariffs by, i don’t know, fifty or a hundred per cent? do we want tariffs that are higher than is necessary to rectify the injury? because that is what removing the lesser duty rule is: higher tariffs than the injury margin. personally i have come to the conclusion that the answer is ‘no’. because i am happy to help the steel industry, but i do not want to punish the car industry. i am happy to help the fertiliser industry, but i do not want to punish the farmers. however, if the commission says that this is not an important principle, parliament will go in the same direction and find exemptions. council has been here for the whole time during this debate, and i would like to say that i am very happy and thankful for this. because it can hear for itself that this is an important question for parliament. i do not know if the council has any comments, because we are eager to debate and discuss this with the council in order to be able to conclude this quickly. maybe the council could indicate whether or not it wants to discuss this as soon as possible.
mr president, the reason why it is complicated to introduce the ftt is that it is complicated to have the ftt. it has been tested. we had it in sweden, and it made financial transactions move to the city of london. today it would make the mayor of shanghai and the financial markets in other parts of the world very happy. there is an error in the description of the financial transaction tax. it is based on a belief that there is some free money lying about in the bank vaults and the financial markets, but that is not true. the financial transaction tax takes aim at those who are saving for their pensions or trying to finance investments in jobs and growth, who are exporting or importing or trying to finance deficits. there is no anonymous market paying for this: it is human beings and companies who are supposed to contribute to the growth, and that is why it is complicated and that is why it has failed in sweden. that is why it is complicated to introduce it now. if we are to have an ongoing debate, do discuss the complications, because the damages are bigger than the potential benefits and we are risking making the situation worse. we need more and better competitiveness in europe, not less. taxes do not create jobs, but investments do. mr president, i would like to thank you. i am glad that after long negotiations we are now going to complete the work on the seasonal worker directive. i would like to thank my colleague claude moraes for a very good job. it is a shame that workers in europe, from fruit growers in the south to berry pickers in the forests in the north, are being exploited by unscrupulous employers and that very few employers can actually be prosecuted and punished when exploitation is revealed. as a social democrat, i am of the opinion that more must be done to protect workers from third countries from exploitation. even if it would have been best to have identical rules for all types of workers, regardless of the type of work they do, i still think that the agreement is a step forward. most importantly, strict equal treatment with workers from the country where the work is carried out applies in terms of pay and working conditions and the rules on sanctions against employers who use seasonal workers are strict. it also introduces the possibility of imposing multi-level liability, so that subcontractors can also be punished. nor do they prevent member states from introducing more favourable rules. now we will have to put pressure on the member states to ensure that these rules are properly implemented and that no loopholes are exploited.

madam president, in europe we need workers from third countries to sustain our economy and it is very important that they have decent work. that is why i have supported the seasonal workers directive because it offers the possibility of improving the protection of workers coming from third countries for temporary work. at the same time, it does not change the laws of the member states and does not affect member states’ ability to decide how many workers to welcome and admit, depending on the needs of their national labour markets. the directive also gives workers more flexibility to change employer during a maximum period of nine months, and this is a good provision for the workers. it is very important to ensure the equal treatment of workers from third countries and national workers in the same sector, in order to avoid exploitation. i welcome this step forward. i would like to call on the presidency to move forward too on the other two important aspects of the migration package: the intra-corporate transfers directive and the question of the admission of student researchers and trainees, which are also very important for european economies.

in writing. - air travel is vital to our economy, both for tourism and for jobs, and affects many of us in our daily lives. but delays, lack of information, and varied business practices between airlines often cause irritation and anxiety among travelers. i therefore support the new rules for air passengers to improve conditions for consumers. we have now struck a good balance between clearer rules on complaints and compensation, while taking account of the difficult economic situation of the airline industry. among other things, we clarify the definition of 'extraordinary circumstances' so that it cannot be misused as an excuse for not compensating travellers. in addition, we are increasing the requirements for information from airlines on delays and baggage costs. thanks to compensation thresholds and the prohibition of the 'no show policy', we are increasing consumer protection. at the same time, i urge consumers to be responsible and, for example, to inform airlines when they do not use a route in their booking.
in writing. - the christian democrats believe that it is reasonable to have three binding targets for reducing greenhouse gas emissions, the share of renewable energy and increasing energy efficiency. however, the binding targets are entirely dependent on the distribution key between the member states. in sweden, almost 51% of energy production is already generated from renewable sources. to a large extent, we owe this to our waterways. other countries have a very different situation. in poland, renewable energy sources account for 8%. the situation is similar in other eu countries. in 2011, the polish deputy prime minister said that coal power is the future. the overall picture suggests that when the allocation key is decided later this year, sweden will probably continue to have to bear a large reduction in emissions, even though we already have a high share of renewable energy sources. that is why i voted in favour of a compromise proposal on two binding targets: at least 40% reduction in greenhouse gas emissions and a combined target on renewable energy and efficiency, which the countries themselves decide to allocate. climate is going to be the focus, and then the countries that emit the most carbon dioxide have to change their production. that should be the objective, but it is one that is at risk of being missed by staring blindly at percentages rather than looking at what is most effective.
in writing. - it is important for the stability of europe's banking sector that a common banking union and a common crisis fund can be established, and we believe that sweden should be part of this union in the long term. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover the whole of the european union. we want to emphasise that we are against the use of the eu budget or national budgets to rescue banks in crisis. swedish or other national taxpayers will not be responsible for losses in foreign banks if we join the banking union. each eu country has a responsibility to manage its own public finances, as does the banks' crisis fund, which is to be financed by the banks themselves. rather than draining sovereign finances or increasing speculation, a crisis fund of banks' own money should encourage long-term thinking and enable a more stable financial market. for these reasons we voted in favour of the european parliament's amendments to the commission's proposal for a regulation.

the rapporteur. - madam speaker, please. let me first of all express my warm thanks for the cooperation of the commission in this matter, commissioner barnier and all his colleagues who have been very, very pleasant to work with throughout the process. i would also like to thank the members of this parliament from all the groups for the confidence they have shown in me and the spirit of cooperation which has characterized our cooperation. we have managed very quickly, in record time, to deal with a very large and very complex subject and to bring it to a unanimous vote in the committee on legal affairs. our objective, and mine in particular, was always to reach an agreement at first reading with the council. thanks to the work of all the shadow rapporteurs and other colleagues, we in this parliament have really done everything we can to try to achieve that objective. thank you to all of you who have contributed to this. but unfortunately the council of ministers has not been able to reach a common position and we have therefore not been able to open negotiations between our institutions. although, of course, there has been a lot of work in the council, it is with great, great, great disappointment that i note that the leadership and the willingness to negotiate with parliament that would have been required to bring this matter to a close has been totally lacking. i am sorry to say that we must acknowledge the council's total incompetence and lack of will. as late as december i assumed that there was a good chance of reaching agreement at first reading, but now that chance has been squandered because of a lack of leadership from the other institutions and i am the first to regret that. i shall not go into the details of the proposal because we all know them very well by now. the idea behind this process is now to modernise and improve a system of trademark protection which is basically working well. we pour a drop of oil into the machinery to modernize this; it's all very well balanced and there's a need for it. the proposal before the house contains several improvements both in terms of the governance of ohim and in substantive matters, such as ensuring that freedom of expression is protected against attacks on the basis of trademarks. as rapporteur, i was very pleased that we were able to get unanimous support in the committee. we had important and good discussions between the groups, everyone had to give and take and finally we reached a good compromise that everyone could live with. it was therefore with great disappointment that i noted that the s&d and epp shadow rapporteurs did not respect an agreement that had been reached. we have held hands, and i thought that the cards were in the deck and that an agreement was in place, but apparently that is not the case and i am the first to regret that. the committee had reached a balanced position which would have improved the possibilities of combating counterfeit goods in transit through european ports and airports on their way to other countries, while at the same time we would respect international commitments to allow free transit for goods which are not counterfeit. the compromise included safeguards to guarantee the free transit of generic medicines, including those bound for developing countries. all this work is now suggested by the two major groups in this parliament that we should literally throw it overboard. the issue of access to medicines is of course primarily about patents and not about trademarks, but if, like the epp and the s&d, a definition of trademarks is made that is so broad, it will risk creating problems for the trade in generic medicines, as also noted by médecins sans frontières. instead of supporting the balanced line which we unanimously voted for in the committee, marielle gallo and bernhard rapkay have, after intense industry lobbying, chosen to support an extreme proposal supported by the luxury industry and the big pharmaceutical companies. i'm very, very, very upset about this and i just wonder: it is not that you should have voted for proposals which would have been ineffective in stopping counterfeiting, is it? you don't think we're in danger of throwing our agreement into the sea? you don't want this in your heart, do you?
for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. brands are basically a good thing. they are about protecting consumers and making it clear who is responsible for the product they buy. that's good enough. but unfortunately there is some abuse of trademark rights where trademark owners have targeted artists and other creators of culture when they have used the trademarks, not to pretend to sell a product under that name, but just to use them in art. that is why i was very pleased with what we reached in the committee and the compromise which made it clear that trademarks should not be used to stifle cultural expressions. i was going to recommend my group to vote in favour of this compromise, but as rapporteur cecilia wikström says mrs wikström has done an extremely good job in moving these negotiations forward i also had the impression that we were all in agreement, and so i am extremely surprised and extremely disappointed at the two large groups epp and s&d when they come up at the last minute with an amendment which clearly, as i understand from marielle gallos' comments, is intended to tighten up the rules on goods in transit. we've been talking about this for a very long time. i thought we had an agreement, and now there's a last-minute amendment that's gonna tear the foundation of this compromise. i call on the strongest epp and s&d groups to withdraw these amendments. if they pass tomorrow, i don't see how i can recommend my group to support this package, because this is not the way to do it. if you have an agreement in a negotiation, you should respect it.
the rapporteur. - madam speaker, please. yes, i must emphasize once again that i find it very sad that our cooperation in the committee should end like this. i'm used to people trusting each other and keeping their word. i would like to stress that the epp/s&d amendment risks creating major problems for legitimate international trade and it could also threaten the development of our european ports and airports, which we need for future growth and development. these risks are being taken despite the fact that there was a unanimous proposal in the committee which allows for an effective fight against counterfeiting. the committee also welcomes the commission's proposal to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services, including the carrier's own transport. the amendments have removed all actual protection for these, and instead have put very vague wording in an amendment. now i call on everyone to vote no to this amendment and instead to support our compromise which we agreed on as recently as two months ago. since then, nothing new has happened, except that lobbyists have been running around here and managing to come up with some pretty dirty arguments that people have bought. i'm sorry about that. but overall, i'm satisfied with our fine cooperation. parliament has worked effectively to improve and modernise trade mark protection in europe. there is no need to weaken the report by running through lobbyists' cases for five minutes in 12 and adding ill-conceived amendments, but instead we could vote in favour of the report being adopted unanimously in committee, and we would have a clear and unambiguous line and maintain our confidence and cooperation. something that is needed in europe, especially in our time.
the rapporteur. - mr. speaker, i have to go. yes, if this directive is adopted, it will create improved rules for third country nationals coming to the eu to study, research or do paid or unpaid internships or volunteer work, or simply to work as au pairs. the aim is clearly to make the eu a world centre for study and research, and we must remember that those who are au pairs today are the researchers of tomorrow. in fact, despite the economic downturn in many member states and despite high levels of unemployment, many companies still have difficulty recruiting skilled workers. we in the eu are also facing a difficult demographic challenge with an ageing population, and we can see that we have been an attractive labour market for skilled people from different parts of the world, but that has changed and other countries in other parts of the world are doing better than us at attracting skilled labour, while our sometimes complicated bureaucracy and the growing scepticism in many member states towards immigrants can scare people away. it is important that we now improve the situation and provide clear, simple rules to increase the attractiveness of the eu. more foreign students, researchers and international exchanges will lead to economic growth and foster innovation, create more jobs in the long term and overall contribute to the competitiveness of our member states. the commission proposal brings together two directives, the directive for researchers and the directive for students, and introduces a wide range of improvements which have proved necessary after assessing the situation in the current regulatory framework, where many rules and conditions are unclear and where there is a great deal of variation between countries. in the course of the work on the report in the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs, we have further improved this directive, and i am proud of that. we want harmonised rules between member states on entry conditions and requirements, and clear rules and opportunities, and intra-eu mobility where people are allowed to move between our member states on the basis of the initial authorisation. the current rules are weak, especially as regards students, and if there is something that is good about the eu and that we really need to pay attention to and offer to others, it is this: mobility. we are introducing time limits: we are giving the member states 30 days to decide on an application and an extension of a time limit in cases where there is an appeal. we also want to see increased access to the labour market, where students will be allowed to work at least 20 hours a week, and after graduation or completion of research, students and researchers will be able to stay for 18 months in the country concerned to look for a job, start a business or otherwise establish themselves in the country. the provision for family members of researchers should also apply to family members of students, as students today are generally older when they complete their higher education. it's not entirely unlikely that they also have a family. the provisions for authorisation shall also be voluntary and not mandatory. i have to say that the negotiations have not gone very well and that the council has not yet reached a common position. this means that it will not be possible to reach an agreement before the end of the mandate. i have therefore decided, with the support of the shadow rapporteurs, to put the report to the vote in plenary in order to adopt parliament's negotiating position and to enable negotiations to begin as soon as possible during the next term of office. i would like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs for the excellent work we have done together and for the very good results we have achieved.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, europe badly needs to attract talent. we need to win the challenge of global competition and attractiveness is a global race. i have therefore been responsible within the ppe – and i am proud and glad of this – for trying to actively engage in finding a solution to attract, train, develop and keep talent in europe to match the needs of our companies. our target has been to have a directive which has the right balance between facilitating and simplifying, and at the same time preventing abuse. i would like here to thank sincerely the rapporteur and commissioner malmström for their excellent cooperation. we have succeeded in overcoming fragmentation between member states, in overcoming the cumbersome, long procedures for admission and entry, and now we have a common, clear and easier rule that gives legal certainty and helps to prevent abuse. i would like to be clear here – this partly in response to my colleague mr[nbsp ]stoyanov – that we need to attract talent, competence and skills to europe. that will help growth and jobs in europe, not decrease them. we need to have cross-fertilisation, people-to-people contact. innovation and research needs to have new people, more people, including from third countries, but we also need clear rules. that is why in the ppe we have insisted that member states should continue to decide how many entry permits are granted. the national labour laws would apply – this directive will not prejudice the labour markets. so students and researchers would be granted the possibility to work but the rules of the labour market will remain national. they will also have to clearly show that they are able to support themselves and that they will not live on social benefits from member states. i hope that the next parliament will negotiate fast with the member states to be able to implement this directive which is very important for the european economy. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 149(8))
blue-card answer. – i very much agree with you and this has also been the ppe’s line: that by facilitating and simplifying, we want to prevent abuse, and also exploitation. it is also very important that those coming to our countries are treated fairly and in a dignified way. all the statistics, studies and documents show that we need more people from other countries because we have a competence and knowledge-based economy. we need innovation and research and we need cross-fertilisation from other countries. it is precisely for this reason that we have put forward this directive: to prevent abuse. we now have a very clear framework with very clear rules and procedures on what the migrants coming to our countries need to show and to do in order to be admitted. we did not have this before. it is a step forward.
the rapporteur. - madam speaker, please. i think that sometimes you can hear the question between the lines: can we afford to take in researchers and students from third countries? my counter-question is: can we afford not to take them? can we afford not to see that they establish themselves in the eu through research or education? remember, one of these people is going to be part of a research team that solves the energy challenge of the future. one of them will be helping to solve the issue of a cancer for which we have so far found no cure. until now, many, many, hundreds and thousands of researchers have been trained in the eu at a top university every year. they have, at the end of their research, bought a one-way ticket and gone to the united states, to canada, to australia, to india, and now we see, also to china. we cannot afford to continue to provide the highest level of education and research and then just let them go. we must welcome them to stay here, to settle here and work with us all for growth, development and stability in europe, because we live in a global world and we have to adapt to it. this will be a key to success in the eu in the days ahead. i would like to thank you again for the excellent cooperation we have had with the commission and with my shadow rapporteurs, and i look forward to tomorrow's vote. i hope that everyone will vote by pressing plus on the split votes and separate votes that are left, and we will have a report as fine as the next one unanimously adopted by the libe committee just a few months ago.

mr president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteurs for emphasising the need for fiscal consolidation, the need to further boost cost and non-cost competitiveness in the global economy, and the need for ambitious structural reforms. the problem for the european union economy is not, and has never been, fiscal consolidation – it has been the lack of fiscal consolidation. it has been the lack of structural reforms. that has created unemployment and deep social problems. we all know that the policy of ʻspendarityʼ which is advocated from that side has led to ever growing costs for interest rates, squeezing out important spending on welfare and important investments in infrastructure. yet still some of the colleagues here are defending more spending, more deficits, higher interest rates, more cost for interest rates, but every step which is taken in that direction undermines prosperity and competitiveness. what we see now is that those countries which have started to implement fiscal consolidation and structural reforms are emerging from the deeper problems. spending or ʻspendarityʼ caused high unemployment and high interest rates and undermined public finances. the medicine to deal with that cannot be more of the same. the fact is that countries where reforms were carried out were able to stay out of the crisis. countries where reforms were started are coming out of the crisis. but in the countries that persist there are still deep problems. this is why these proposals in the report are so important.

for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. this is a politically sensitive issue, and the question of whether intermediaries should be subject to more extensive prohibitions on commissions has been particularly sensitive. several countries, such as denmark, finland, the netherlands and the united kingdom, have introduced a ban on commissions. the swedish government is currently preparing to ban upfront commissions. there is a risk that insurance intermediaries do not act in the long term. the important thing is that the intermediary always acts in the best interests of the client. but the risk of a ban on commissions is that it leads to a monopoly of insurance intermediation by the major leading insurance companies and banks. member states should therefore be able to go further and introduce more extensive information requirements for insurance intermediaries in relation to commissions and fees than those provided for in this directive. this is important to point out to those countries which feel that stricter rules on commissions and fees are needed. let me continue in a language that perhaps more of you understand. two of the amendments from mr langen concern alignment to the markets in financial instruments directive (mifid). therefore i would like to ask the commissioner and mr langen: what does that mean in practice? because we will hear, and i have heard from my dear friend and colleague here, mr giegold, that amendments 4 and 5 will have no influence on the coming negotiations concerning the alignment of mifid and the insurance mediation directive (imd 2). i have heard the opposite. if we put in all these amendments from mr giegold 8, 9, 10 and 11 this will make the legal procedure more technical and difficult. i would like to hear, commissioner and mr langen, whether amendments 4 and 5 are not enough to secure the alignment of mifid and imd2? this is essential to how i construct the voting list for my group tomorrow.
member of the commission. - madam president, this debate gave us an opportunity to mention a wide range of issues relating to the health sector, and it is right and proper that this debate should take place, because health is an important element in our quality of life. i have always said that the financial and economic crisis should never lead to a health crisis in europe, so when the commission calls for better spending and also sometimes for corrective measures for economic situations which member states have found themselves in independently of the will of the commission, it is in favour of the sustainability of the health system. our health systems, because of demographic trends in particular, are not sustainable unless they are reformed; but how the reform should take place is within the discretion of the member states. of course we can give suggestions. we have set up a panel of experts within the commission to give advice on the sustainability of the health systems. but if we take greece, which was the first to seek assistance, we gave it assistance, and it was also due to the efforts of the commission and the work of the greek government that more people in greece are covered by public health insurance today than they were before. so this goes to prove that the commission is there to help governments. the commission is not a non-governmental organisation, because it has a different role: it is there to help governments. we have helped governments; we shall continue to help governments, and this health programme is a programme to help, principally, governments but also other organisations on particular health projects. i was very pleased to note that a number of meps mentioned the question of health inequalities. incidentally, the commission recently published a report on health inequalities, and one is pleased to note that in certain areas, including some which have been mentioned in this assembly (for instance, infant mortality), the gap has narrowed. if you ask me if i am pleased with the narrowing of the gap, i would say yes, but not enough. the real concern is that, although in certain areas the gap has narrowed within the european union, it has not narrowed between certain income groups, and this is of concern. although overall there was an improvement in certain income groups, the gap has not widened – but neither has it narrowed. i think mental health is assuming even more importance on the political agenda not only of the commission but also of member states, and i was very pleased to note in the last two presidencies that due importance was given to mental health. mental health should not be the cinderella of our health systems. it should be treated like physical health, and i think here much has been done to reduce the stigma relating to mental health itself. a problem which has been mentioned repeatedly by at least four meps was the question of the lack of healthcare professionals, and i would like to refer to what ms gardini and other members said at the beginning. is health to be considered like a financial albatross around governments’ necks, or should it be considered as an opportunity? i firmly believe that it is an opportunity. earlier last year we published a report called ‘investing in health’, because all the studies show that in the coming years until 2020 we will be needing hundreds of thousands, if not millions, of jobs in the health sector in the european union. at the same time we have 25-26% youth unemployment in some member states. so something is wrong, particularly with our education system, which should be tilted in favour of finding employment for people and encouraging them to take up jobs in the health sector, which are available. investing in health also means investing funds in order to reduce expenditure. right now we have established an e-health network within the commission, where governments meet with stakeholders and with the commission itself, not to reduce the level of healthcare in europe but to use the money which we have in a better way. in certain areas, e-health can maintain the same level of health care at a reduced cost. the same applies, for instance, to e-prescriptions, which in greece and portugal, among others, have also reduced expenditure. i was very pleased to note that most member states laid emphasis on the question of prevention. of course this is a very difficult campaign, because if you look at the health budgets, only 3% of the national health budgets in the member states goes to prevention, which is why we are putting the prevention of chronic disease at the forefront of the political agenda. the commission is organising a chronic disease summit just before easter, because this is also a way of reducing costs. we can reduce costs through prevention by fighting the four causes of the most common chronic diseases like diabetes, cardiovascular disease and cancer, which have also been mentioned in this parliament, and by encouraging more physical activity, better nutrition, the reduction of alcohol consumption and, hopefully, the reduction of tobacco consumption. i hope that tomorrow this assembly will approve the tobacco directive, which will help us in reaching our target of reducing the number of smokers in the european union by 2% over the next five years, which means 2.4 million fewer smokers. finally, on the question of solidarity, which was mentioned as regards the 90% average, i would like to say that the original commission proposal was that the 80% co-financing should apply only to member states which have less than 90% of the gni (gross national income) average, but of course the commission proposes, and it is up to co-legislators to approve – and sometimes one has to compromise in this respect. i still believe that, even though i preferred the original proposal, this compromise gives preference to member states which have less than 90%, because even those projects which will be taken over by member states which have above 90% must, in certain conditions and in certain cases, involve the developing economies of the member states with a lower gni. i would like to thank everyone who participated in this debate because, apart from the health programme, we also discussed general health issues, and this gives me a better idea of where the emphasis is being put by the representatives of our citizens. thanks again to madam grossetête and to all the shadow rapporteurs.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, i just want to continue with the thanks, because i am really impressed with what has been done not only by the rapporteur, mr tošenovský, but also by the shadow rapporteurs and all those who were backing the team in strong cooperation. sometimes i have the feeling that time flies. in this case, to be honest, i did not have the feeling that time flies, for we are in a hurry and two and a half years is quite a period; but it can be adopted now, that is the main thing, and let us count our blessings. so the strong cooperation has a result, the child is born. you had a lot of patience throughout the whole process, and it has been a long process. everybody agrees – and that is also a remarkable point – that digital infrastructure matters, and of course sometimes you ask yourself: what is the real question? why does digital infrastructure matter? because it is not just about one sector, and it is quite important to mention that again. it is not only about telecoms, it is not only about ict, it is about the digital industrial world. it is about every sector, so to speak. digital infrastructure underpins everything we do across all aspects of the economy and in our society. we have to make it easier to interact between public administrations, businesses and citizens. we need to deploy, we need to modernise high-speed internet networks, and we must certainly remember that digital services can drive competitiveness and support citizens, improving their daily lives. to do that, we badly need infrastructure at the european level, interconnected and interoperable. but at the moment we do not have it. we have now achieved 100[nbsp ]% broadband – that is quite a remarkable figure – and all eu households now have at least a basic connection. yet i am focused on the next step, which goes beyond the basic: the high-speed networks needed for tomorrow’s services, for europe’s competitiveness and for europe’s growth. that is why we proposed an innovative scheme whereby private investors would take the risk in the knowledge that their investment would be secured by eu public funds: the digital connecting europe facility. the european council – and sometimes you need to be aware that life is not all pleasure – last year drastically reduced the budget for that facility. this happened some time ago, but i still feel sad about it, because it means a big loss for european broadband and european competitiveness, and because it signalled a need to find other ways to secure investments in digital infrastructure. but more worrying was the fact that at that moment the message did not get through. digital technologies are revolutionising our lives and livelihoods, they are helping public services modernise. building the infrastructure of the future today is as critical as transport or energy networks are today. i am absolutely convinced; i know many of you are, too; but others remain to be. we had to fundamentally reorient the digital connecting europe facility. it will now focus on digital service infrastructures and pan-european platforms to support policies from healthcare to heritage, from e-invoicing and cyber security to cross-border public procurement. in spite of the limited budget (being creative we need to think about it), broadband is too important to be dropped altogether, and i think we have a consensus on that. the facility will provide the right structures for investment, set up innovative financial instruments for broadband projects with the european investment bank, and provide dedicated technical assistance to make more projects ready for potential financial assistance. and of course this will only go some way to meet the broadband challenge. private markets will do a lot of the work, and we need to give them what they need, such as a durable framework that rewards investment, and cutting the cost of broadband roll-out and taking down the barriers in our telecoms single market. where the markets can still not deliver, local, regional and national authorities will have to close the financing gap, and i sincerely hope that the structural funds will also help here. on the involvement of a member states expert group, the commission considers that this does not in any way affect our prerogatives or the exercise of our implementing powers. we will add, madam president, a declaration to that effect to the final act. but overall this is a great result, the best we could achieve in the financial climate, and i again thank the rapporteur and his shadows.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, i would like to thank the commissioner and the rapporteur for their very good work. i think it is important to state one thing in particular, and that is that the services of today would not have been possible with the capacity and speeds of yesterday. the changes we have seen were needed for this and by the same token the services of tomorrow, if we are to be in the lead, will not be possible with the capacities and speeds of today. that is why we need to raise standards and set them higher. i think that what is very good in this report is that we are paving the way for doing so. due to monies being reduced there are no longer the same opportunities as were planned for from the beginning, but what we can do is, in a focused way, to set the higher standards or the higher speeds that we need to deploy as soon as possible. what we can do is to ensure that we can have the pilot projects showing that this is not futuristic, it is realistic, and what we can do is to encourage private investors to follow suit because then there will be a change. and i would like to emphasise to the commission and to the commissioner that we need to proceed rapidly regarding all areas of broadband because they are crucial to economic growth in our societies. this is one of the few chances we have to create good opportunities for a new and modernistic wave of growth in our european societies but also to bind the european economy together to the biggest market in the world. the digital economy is the economy that will be the most competitive one and we have paved the way for it, and i would like to thank everyone involved in that.

in writing. - we swedish social democrats abstained in the vote on the approval of an agreement between the european union and turkey on the readmission of persons without residence permits. we support closer relations between turkey and the eu and we see the value of cooperation on readmission issues. given the conflict in syria and the large flows of refugees from there, we cannot at the moment support a readmission agreement that reduces the legal pathways for refugees to europe and has a negative impact on the possibilities of applying for asylum.
in writing. - we swedish social democrats are in favour of investing in rail transport for the sake of the environment and to make it easier and faster to travel by train in europe. the railway market in europe needs to be coordinated more than it is today, for example with better integrated information and ticketing systems, and safety must be at its highest. however, we do not believe that a common railway area based on deregulation and competition can solve the problems we face. sweden and the united kingdom are two bad examples of railway deregulation in europe. the railways of the eu do not need more market thinking. instead, increased investment in infrastructure and a sound maintenance of existing tracks are needed. crossborder rail transport for both freight and passengers also needs to be greatly improved. a functioning cohesive railway that is kept free from market experimentation is important both for employment and for reducing the climate impact in the eu.
in writing. - the fourth railway package aims to open up the passenger transport market also in those eu countries which, unlike sweden, still have closed markets. we believe that the european railway market would benefit from being opened up and exposed to competition. this would lead to better efficiency and lower prices, and also create new opportunities for swedish companies to compete in other countries. the european parliament's vote, despite our opposition, adopted several amendments which will restrict effective competition in the railway sector and undermine the development of an internal railway market. since this sends the wrong signal to the railway sector and runs counter to the open market we believe in, we could not vote in favour of these parts of the package.

mr president, i would like to thank you. today we voted for eu-wide rules that affect us all and that can save lives. thanks to a mandatory e-call system in all new car models from october 2015, your car will automatically call the emergency number 112 if you crash. this can, especially in rural areas, reduce ambulance response times by up to 50 percent. in sweden we have long distances and many slums and the e-call system can be vital if a person becomes unconscious and can neither call the emergency services himself nor with help. i want to be clear: we have also strengthened the protection of personal integrity and privacy. it can't be possible to follow your car. the information about your crash site is only sent if you crash. at the same time, we have common standards in europe that help make it work. we have fought for road safety and data protection, and that is welcome.
in writing. - i wholeheartedly support the goal of increasing accessibility to public authorities' websites. it is important that websites are adapted so that everyone, including people with disabilities, blindness and visual impairments, has access to information. however, i am not convinced that legislation at eu level is the right way to achieve results. much is already underway in the member states and there is a risk that extensive eu rules will create additional costs and close the door to innovative solutions. it is doubtful whether it would add value to detail the design of websites of state authorities, municipalities and county councils as well as state and municipal companies, associations and foundations throughout the eu. a more reasonable alternative would be, for example, to highlight the key services to be available and to ensure that public authorities improve the implementation of the strategies already in place in the member states. i am in favour of non-discrimination and greater transparency, but i have abstained on this report the way forward is not necessarily rules at eu level.
