mr president, i would like to thank you. we are now debating one of the most important issues for the european union. remember, it was founded on the principle of the equal worth and fundamental rights of all people. eu membership requires fulfilling the copenhagen criteria, which require, among other things, a stable democracy and respect for fundamental human rights and freedoms. in addition, minorities and their rights must be protected. but once you are a member, there is no mechanism to hold you accountable if you do not meet these founding commitments. in the last few years, when we have suffered from the economic crisis, we have seen that one country after another has violated fundamental rights, but the criticism is not as harsh and the measures are not as visible as if the economic stability pact had been violated. why don't we take this as seriously as we do? remember that the eu exists to secure the rights of its citizens, not the ability of its member states to violate the fundamental rights of their citizens. in recent years we have seen examples of member states violating the charter of fundamental rights. the clearest example is, of course, hungary, where they have set up a state-controlled media council - nothing more than a large censorship authority - where they have discriminated against minorities, restricted the independence of the judiciary and other things. with right-wing extremism on the rise, we have seen the erosion of basic rights in even more countries. it is therefore high time that an independent commission or mechanism was set up to scrutinise all member states and to propose sanctioning authorities when necessary. the violation of fundamental values must have consequences, both economic and political. i welcome this report which so clearly supports fundamental rights and ensures their observance, and i urge you all to support louis michel's report wholeheartedly, which is extremely important. *the speaker agreed to answer a question (blue card) in accordance with rule 149 (8) of the rules of procedure).
blue-card answer. – i would be happy to furnish the answer to this question. first of all, the media council that they put in place is nothing but a big censoring authority. secondly, to have forced retirements on judges and prosecutors constitutes an undermining of the principle of the rule of law in a democracy in a european member state. hungary is a tremendous example of where fundamental rights have not at all been respected as they should have been, and for that hungary needs to take responsibility.
mr president, i would like to thank you. unlike the watered-down version of the epp, i think this report is a good overview of the situation of fundamental rights in the eu in 2012. it contains a number of good sections on sexual and reproductive rights, media diversity, the situation of roma, the rights of asylum seekers and the rights of lgbt people. it also has an unusually strong focus on trade union and social rights, which i very much welcome as a social democrat. as rapporteur for eu legislation on trafficking in human beings, i also welcome the call to member states which have not yet fully implemented the directive to do so. unfortunately, the report contains very problematic texts on drug policy in paragraph 105. this paragraph seems to me to be very oddly placed in a report on fundamental rights. it is a very sweeping and vague criticism, and it is completely unfounded in the facts of the drug situation in the member states. given the enormous suffering and harm caused by drugs, the goal for me can never be anything other than a society completely free of drugs. that is why i shall vote against that point.
mr president, i fully support the european investigation order. what is it? it gives a member state the possibility to ask another member state to carry out investigation measures to resolve serious crime. to win our battle against human trafficking, child pornography, drug smuggling, terrorism or cybercrime and counterfeiting, we need to work closely together. criminality has become more and more complex, sophisticated and mobile. we should give better tools to law enforcement authorities to prosecute criminals, organised crime and criminal networks when they move cross-border. this is about recognising the action of other member states and helping each other to carry out investigations. national competences in law enforcement are fully respected, and national criminal law will continue to apply. the existing rules on police and judicial cooperation are simplified. it will be easier for our police and our prosecutors to operate efficiently. for instance, the introduction of clear time limits will speed up collection of crucial evidence from other member states. it is also important that we introduce clear safeguards to protect the fundamental rights of suspects and clear rules on remedies. increased cooperation between member states is essential to effectively fight cross-border crime. the european investigation order and the european arrest warrant are good examples of what we can do together at european level to really benefit our european citizens. with a european investigation order we increase the safety of our citizens, while at the same time respecting their integrity. this is an important step forward towards creating an area of freedom and security in europe, as called for in the stockholm programme.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, in september[nbsp ]2012 the amount of ice in the arctic sea reached the lowest ever recorded by satellites. it was then about half the size of the average minimum from 1981 to 2010. ice covers a considerable part of the maritime zone around svalbard most of the time but now that is changing. if you go nasa’s website you can see how the ice is melting away at a frightening speed and new waters are opening up for transport, oil, gas exploration and fishing. of course this attracts a lot of interest from others. considering the different interpretations of the svalbard treaty dating from 1920, i am not surprised by the initiative by some colleagues to have this debate here in parliament today. but my concern is not the interests of fishing nations but the preservation of what is still pristine nature up there. the waters under the ice cap have not been accessible for more than 800[nbsp ]000 years, and of course we must take the responsibility before it is too late to protect these sensitive ecosystems. as is often the case, norway has a particular role to play in all of this. this week there is a meeting going on in greenland between the five arctic nations. during this meeting canada, denmark and the us are trying to convince russia and norway to establish a high seas arctic fishing moratorium, something norway has shown very little interest in. i am very surprised by that. i respect a lot of norwegian fisheries policies and of course norway is a neighbouring country to my country, sweden. but i would say that if any claims from the norwegian side on taking responsibility for the fishing protection zone around svalbard are going to be taken seriously by environmentalists – and maybe also by the eu – i suggest that norway sides with those arctic democracies that want to have a fishing moratorium on the high seas, and not side again with russia to exploit what should be left alone.

in writing. - we see private copying compensation as an important tool in cultural policy. in essence, this remuneration promotes a rich cultural life by ensuring that authors receive fair compensation for the private copying of their works. the private copying levy maintains a reasonable balance between the interests of consumers and authors. we note that private copying compensation schemes do not always work optimally in practice. there is therefore every reason to ensure that they are continuously developed and modernised. however, their total abolition is not appropriate at this stage. this is still the only solution available that preserves the essential balance between consumers and authors. no credible alternative solutions have been presented. whatever developments in this area may take place in the future, it is vital that personal privacy is safeguarded.

deputising for the rapporteur. - mr president, apologies from mr kalfin, who cannot be here, so he has asked me to step in for him. first of all, let me express the gratitude of the rapporteur over the fact that an agreement on this very important issue was actually reached in such a short time. we had worries that it might take longer, but it is now in place. this will give the european investment bank the possibility to continue its operations without disruption, which is of course extremely important. the rapporteur also very much welcomes the agreement to keep the lending ceilings at current levels. that is one of the things the european parliament has actually fought for. the use of eu funds through the eib allows for a leverage effect of more funding, sometimes up to 25 times the amount we invest. that is a very efficient use of scarce eu resources. the rapporteur also thanks the council – in particular the lithuanian presidency, with which he has negotiated – for accepting to use external assigned revenues from the fiduciary account established for the facility for euro-mediterranean investment and partnership, to finance the additional eur[nbsp ]2 billion necessary for the restoration of the ceilings, as suggested by parliament. two billion might not sound very much, but if we consider what these funds can mobilise overall, it is very significant. from the agreement reached it is, however, clear that the decision to use reflows in this way is a one-off deal and cannot be used as a precedent in other contexts. the rapporteur is also very satisfied with the open and constructive work he has had with civil society during the work on of this report. ngos were particularly helpful regarding transparency and on social and environmental aspects, etc. in accordance with the treaty, the eib should endeavour to support the objectives of the union’s development cooperation policy, such as by reducing poverty through inclusive growth and sustainable economy and environmental and social development. the eib should also always make it a priority to strengthen the local private sector in beneficiary countries. there are a number of points which the rapporteur wants to emphasise. in the period from 2014 to 2020, the eib is encouraged to continue pursuing its greener path, by strengthening its emissions performance standards and launching a public review of the climate impact of its lending portfolio. the eib should also support investment projects in sustainable energy security and energy infrastructure, including transmission infrastructure, in particular interconnections that facilitate the integration of electricity from renewable sources. he would also like to emphasise that investments in the field of renewable energy and better energy efficiency should be preferred to investments in fossil fuels. financial agreements with individual promoters relating to eib operations should also include appropriate environmental, social, human rights and labour provisions. we should make an evaluation of the social impact of its operation, and human rights considerations will have a more central role in the bank’s lending. we also emphasise increased transparency, monitoring of the bank’s activities, democratic scrutiny and zero tolerance of tax evasion and fraud as being crucial. we emphasise dialogue with stakeholders and better alignment with the political goals of the european union in the respective regions, and the rapporteur hopes that the eib will continue to work successfully outside the eu as a top-rated bank and as an eu institution. we expect that the board of the eib will strengthen the administrative capacity of the bank in order to be able to accomplish all the new tasks. finally, the rapporteur wants to thank all the shadow rapporteurs, the rapporteurs of the other committees, and the staff of the committee on budgets and the political groups for the excellent work on this dossier.
deputising for the rapporteur. - mr president, on behalf of the rapporteur mr kalfin (i am just replacing him), let me make a few comments. first of all, thanks to the commissioner, because what he said on the external mandate makes it possible for me to be much shorter. i completely agree with his arguments when it comes to investments outside europe. just think: is it good or bad for europe to have a more stable environment in ukraine, in north africa, etc.? of course it is good for europe. if we do not do it for humanitarian reasons or solidarity reasons, we should do it anyway for economic reasons, because they would be stable partners for us in trade and investments, etc. it is good for europe to help stability through investments in our neighbouring countries, that is for sure. that is my first point. secondly, one of the things we have tried to do with the european parliament report here is to improve the operations of the european investment bank (eib) through increased transparency, better monitoring, more impact assessments and really establishing the zero tolerance principle when it comes to tax evasion and fraud, etc. that would also be important for the future: to enhance credibility in those instruments. finally, my third point. it has been said now, when the financial framework and the eu budget has been cut, ‘let us put more emphasis on these instruments’. yes, but they cannot really replace the budget instruments. they are always a complement. they are a good, strong complement, but we really need to fight for the european union budget as well, which for the moment has big problems – as has been pointed out by helga trüpel and others – when it comes to payments for humanitarian aid, etc. we need to have both inside the union – i am the rapporteur in parliament for project bonds and i know their importance, but they cannot replace grants and investments directly from the budget. let us have both, because these can also be very useful.

in writing. - i voted in favour of the report to improve equality between women and men in the eu because we still have such a long way to go to break the glass ceiling and we still have a considerable pay gap between women and men for the same job. promoting equality is also important to combat violence against women, to change the culture and the attitudes and to eliminate gender stereotypes. we need to join forces to empower women and increase their representation in politics and in economic life in all sectors. however, decisions concerning gender quotas or family policies should be taken at national level, not at eu level.
in writing. - we voted today in favour of the report on equality between women and men in 2012. the report addresses a number of issues which have serious subsidiarity problems, such as pay, pensions and the design of training and school plans. we do not want the eu to have competence in this area, but we have chosen to support the report to draw attention to the problems of gender equality in europe.
in writing. - when the european parliament voted today on the report on equality between women and men in the european union 2012, i chose to vote against. women and men should be equal and have the same opportunities, in the family as in society in general. this requires the removal of structures, attitudes and traditions that impede and prevent genuine gender equality. we all have a responsibility for this. the work is very much about opinion-forming and attitude-changing, but legislating for gender equality through, among other things, gender quota systems and compulsory parental leave, as the report proposes, is the wrong way to go. i also think that the rapporteur is wrong to talk about what may or may not be included in textbooks, as the national curricula are and will remain a matter for the member states to decide. and as the report states, it is absurd to blame the eu's austerity policies and the troika for the widening gap between men and women. we all have a responsibility to work for gender equality, even in times of crisis.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the report on public access to documents of the eu institutions. we need to send a strong message to citizens that we are committed to openness and transparency, that we want them to know what we are doing here. i am not convinced, however, that the establishment of new eu bureaucracy, such as the transparency officer or the independent eu oversight authority proposed in the report, will be an added value for more transparency. we need a more proactive approach to transparency from all the eu institutions, in particular the council and the commission. the way forward is to promote a strong political will for a culture of openness in a constructive way.
in writing. - i voted yes. the report raises a very important issue. if you are for human rights, you must be against all forms of torture. torture must be fought wherever it exists in the world. the report also stresses the importance of eu embassies and their increased role as global actors. it is to present the eu as a state entity. i am opposed to the eu's development into a federal global superpower. i do not share the federalist positions on these points in the report.
madam president, i would like to thank you. it is now 40 years since the first automatic data processing law came into force in sweden, which gave citizens a certain level of protection. it is time for the eu to renew its legislation and to ensure that it is passed, and the matter is no more complex or difficult in the council. it gives us the right to move data, to choose who we want to have our services. it gives us the right to have our data erased and it gives us the right to express consent. today, if you were to read all the terms and conditions of the apps and programs and things that you use, it would take 180 hours a year. we want to help citizens through simple symbols where you get the right to know why your data is being collected. you have the right to know whether they are being kept, whether they are being treated differently from their original purpose, whether they are being given to third parties or rented out. we are giving rights, so it is wrong for the council to block this development.
madam president, with this important reform we will increase personal data protection from account fragmentation between the member states and provide legal certainty. this regulation affects every aspect of our lives and concerns both consumers and companies, the way we access goods and services and communicate on social media, deal with marketing, get information and book travel. that is why i have been pushing to make it applicable in real life, with a clear definition of consent and with profiling that is technologically neutral and future-proof. we succeeded in striking a balance between higher standards of privacy, public access to documents, and freedom of expression. i am happy that my proposals to protect children have been included. information should be child-friendly and easy to understand, because teenagers may not be aware of the consequences and just ‘click it away’. to ensure constant verification, the industry should take its share of the responsibility. there are still, however, issues that need to be improved. the current definition of sensitive data may affect the swedish health register, and unless further exceptions to the right of erasure are made, medical research will be hampered. we also need to cut red tape for companies in order to really make sure that the development of innovation and a competitive digital single market can blossom. together we should move quickly in implementing negotiations with the council and improve the regulation to make it even more applicable.
blue-card question to sarah ludford. – baroness ludford, i was a bit surprised to hear you say that you were opposing the european cloud. before i became a politician i was a computer programmer, so i am wondering, could you please explain to me, if we continue to rely on us infrastructure and if the data is stored and processed in the us, how will you technically be able to prevent the nsa from simply reading whatever they want with no effort at all?
mr president, i would like to thank you. one of the things we're going to vote on tomorrow is whether or not to urge european governments to offer edward snowden protection and asylum. i ask all colleagues to support that demand. edward snowden is a hero. he has uncovered a gigantic spy system that is beyond anything george orwell could have imagined in his worst nightmares. it's never stopped a single terrorist attack, but it costs billions and it violates our human rights. this is not acceptable. the united states should be our friend, not our big brother. mass surveillance is not something that protects our democratic society. it's a direct threat to it. edward snowden is a hero, and it's not just our duty to give him asylum and protection. it would be an honor.
madam president, cross-border mobile crime is growing in europe, and it is crucial to increase cooperation at european level to fight cross-border crime effectively. there should not be any place where criminals can hide in any corner of europe. the mandate of the european public prosecutor should be limited to fraud against the financial interests of the european union, and that is good. however, the commission proposal raises serious concerns regarding the division of responsibility between the member states and the european union, and its added value is questionable. the european public prosecutor’s office (eppo) is intrusive in national legal systems because it harmonises some of the sensitive areas of criminal law such as the admissibility of evidence or procedures for remedy against decisions taken by the public prosecutorʼs office. the confusion of competences between national authorities and the european prosecutor undermines legal certainty. the whole structure is not clear. how would it work in reality with national delegates? investigations should continue to be carried out by national prosecutors to avoid any overlap that could undermine justice. clear safeguards for the rights of suspects and data protection should be defined. i therefore call on the commission to reconsider its position and to present a new proposal that respects subsidiarity better. the fact that 14 national parliaments have issued a yellow card procedure cannot be ignored.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this package travel report is so important and could have been so good if people had really been put before the market. we social democrats have indeed tried to improve consumer protection for package travel buyers, but have not received sufficient support on the matter. the proposal does not sufficiently improve consumer protection for package travel buyers. unfortunately, it can be seen that this would actually worsen the situation in some member states compared to the present situation. firstly, the report risks undermining a number of national consumer rights. secondly, the report does not take sufficient account of new business models, which is worrying for the future. the proposal also introduces the possibility of limiting the compensation to be paid to travellers in cases where the package is found to be of inferior quality or value. in addition, the ceiling for possible price increases has been removed, which is not good from the point of view of consumer rights. i hope, however, that the report will improve along the way and that the new parliament and council will succeed in getting a more consumer-friendly report. i know i sound negative. i would like to conclude by urging you to vote in favour of amendment no 100, which gives travellers the right to terminate the contract before departure in the event of extraordinary events, such as illness, death in the family or an accident.
in writing. - food has many dimensions. overproduction and food waste are major environmental problems, and overconsumption at the individual level, especially of unhealthy food, imposes high costs on society and causes great suffering and premature death for many individuals. i am happy to support ideas that promote a healthier diet and therefore voted in favour of this non-legislative report, even though one paragraph mentioned the cultural value of wine, something we do not think should be in a report that deals with the importance of healthier food and drink.

in writing. - i abstained on the directive on the protection of personal data processed by law enforcement authorities. while it is important to have high standards of data protection too when it is the police and the judiciary which process our data, i think that these rules are too restrictive and they risk undermining the important work of the police and the judiciary to prevent and combat crime. for example, it would impossible to use important investigation tools such as dna comparisons, because of the far reaching restrictions on the use of genetic data. we need to strike a better balance between privacy and integrity in order to protect citizens and let the police do their job, which is to serve and protect citizens. member states have different criminal and procedural laws which vary considerably. to harmonise data protection rules in this field is very intrusive in the different national legal systems and raises serious concerns of subsidiarity.

for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. climate change is real. the mackerel war is one of the first concrete examples of the resource conflicts that we will see more and more of. natural disasters, sea level rise, water scarcity, animal and plant species moving or dying out - all of this will put humanity to the test, not least our ability to show solidarity with each other, to find fair mechanisms to help those affected and to share the resources we have. so how have we managed to resolve the conflict that arose from the mackerel's move north? not very well so far, i must say. in fact, so bad that in 2013 the international council for the exploration of the sea concluded that it is no longer possible to make a reliable stock estimate based on the poor data now available. only two fleets have reported the amount of slippage. according to the international council for the exploration of the sea, the effect of the unreliable data available, leading to ever-increasing errors in stock estimates, is now far beyond what has been understood. consequently, ices has not been able to provide a value for sustainable msy this year. i am in favour of the eu finding a just solution with iceland, norway and the faroe islands, but at the same time i call on all parties involved to take responsibility for the most important party in this conflict, namely the mackerel itself. a stock that was in a really bad way in the '70s, and it could be that way again. we must be clear that we are now in a situation where ices has not been able to give reliable advice on sustainable fish mortality. then it's everyone's responsibility to hold back. but i think some people should be more restrained than others. the fleets that have not even been able to provide accurate data on their catches and discard levels should not have access to the stock. the principle should be no data, no catch.

writer. - mr. speaker, i have to go. the sinai desert is a lawless area. men, women and children are falling victim to human traffickers in the sinai desert as they flee abuses in eritrea in particular. every day innocent people are tortured and murdered. those who come out alive carry damage for life. the kidnapping, torture and murder itself take place outside the european union, but there is a possibility of influence. a recent report from europol last week proves that many of the kidnapped have family in europe. in my home country of sweden alone, hundreds of families have been affected. they can pay as much as $200,000 to get a relative free. i think that my own government, which i support, must act. europol should be given a mandate immediately to be used to set up a so-called 'joint investigation team'. europe can act. member states can act. egypt can and must act too. above all, a new democratic government is needed in eritrea.
the rapporteur. - mr. speaker, i have to go. this regulation captures an idea that is very simple in its essence. the e-id used by citizens in, for example, sweden to declare their income, to apply for student loans or parental insurance or to change their address should also work in other eu countries. just as my passport is a paper-based identity document recognised throughout europe, so too my electronic identity document should be usable throughout europe. that sounds simple and reasonable. anyone who has delved into all the details knows that this is easier said than done. there's some sort of discussion over there, or...? as i said, it sounds simple and reasonable, but anyone who has studied all the details knows that it is, after all, quite difficult to implement. but now we have an agreement with the council and we are going to vote on it during this session. the new law covers a number of different things, but the most important is still the practical benefit for the citizens of europe, as we increasingly deal with our contacts with governments and businesses via the internet. although there are very big differences - as the previous debates have shown - we still live a large part of our lives on the internet. this e-id will make it easier for those contacts. the second part of the act develops the already existing e-signature directive. electronic signatures and electronic seals, among other things, will make it easier for people and businesses to trade and do business across europe. if a company in the northernmost part of sweden - let us say kiruna, which is an hour and 20 minutes' flight north of the capital stockholm - wants to take part in a public procurement procedure in, say, italy or germany, then it can participate in that procedure via the internet by means of an electronic signature. it's a very important win. so what works in the paper world should also work in the online world. i think that is a good thing and we in the european parliament have also succeeded in focusing on some particularly important aspects. firstly, we have been able to implement the legislation quickly. the member states naturally want time to change course, but we have found a good compromise. the recognition of e-ids will now be voluntary for member states from 2015 and compulsory from 2018. for some countries it will be difficult, but it can be done. it's going to work! secondly: subsidiarity is important and therefore it has also been important to ensure that the national e-id systems are respected. sweden has its way, belgium has its way, spain has its way. the most important thing is that the systems can work together, that they can talk to each other and understand each other, so to speak. we have now reached agreement on that too. thirdly: we've strengthened the writings on data protection and privacy, which is extremely important. fourthly, the commission the private sector can be included in the e-id system, and not just the public sector, which a number of member states were totally committed to from the outset. in short, this is a regulation that takes things a few steps forward. it can contribute to jobs and growth, it will certainly do so to some extent and it will be able to make everyday life a little less complicated for us citizens. it makes online payments and e-commerce safer and easier for people and businesses.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, i think it is important to state that the internet has been one of the most dynamic phenomena of the new world. it is fairly young and it is still developing. i am always struck by all those who want to block it from one side or another. there are states which want to block it, sometimes even operators want to block some services and now we have a discussion about blocking a number of services from a legal point of view. i think the important thing is that the internet must be free. that does not mean that everything is free just because it is on the internet. we need to be able to buy products, pay for services, pay for media, pay for a number of things, and we must be open-minded about new services. i do not want any operator to block or discriminate against services on the internet because then you are discriminating against the users, but i do want an opportunity for all those service providers to contribute to the development of the internet. this is the issue about finding the right balance because if we do not allow for special services we undermine a number of future opportunities and possibilities of the internet. then we are reducing it, we are reducing the freedom of the internet because it is a two-way issue – those who deliver and those who use – and we must meet on an open and free internet but with the ability to invest and purchase new things. i want us to increase the capacities and the speeds because that is the main issue, but i want us also to be firm that all new services, be they specialised services or the services that we know are emerging all the time, shall have the freedom of the internet. but it is not free if we are blocking the new services and that is why i think this compromise is of huge importance.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i welcome the agreement reached by the committee on economic and monetary affairs on the payment services directive. first, it implies that third parties are subject to a general regulatory framework in which the rules of the game are clear and fair for all. this is a reasonable and effective model. secondly, it means that the possibility of charging additional fees for card payments will be severely restricted throughout europe. this will save many consumers from the unreasonable charges that are now unfortunately quite common. thirdly, the position of consumers is strengthened in a few other respects. all of these are steps in the right direction. i think we have a good text to vote on tomorrow. i would also like to thank the rapporteurs who have done a good job. thank you so much!
mr president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to begin by thanking catherine ashton for all she has done, both in the enlargement process and in the leadership of the foreign service. the whole idea of a common foreign and security policy came about after we had to deal with the crisis and the wars in the balkans in the 1990s. at that time, we had no common solutions. we had to manage and mobilize our resources not only in diplomacy but also in economics and humanitarian aid. then other crises came and other lessons were learned and the member states were better able to coordinate. the european external action service is a result of these lessons. but the recent crisis in ukraine has shown that the eu must be even better at coordinating its interests and its foreign and defence policy. the lisbon treaty has also given the european parliament greater transparency in the eu's external relations, but it is ultimately the member states that decide on concrete measures such as sanctions. but we also need to be better at engaging in an active dialogue with our citizens and civil society. without this dialogue, it will be difficult to explain and defend our values. the development in our neighborhood should come as no surprise. we've been getting signals for quite some time, but we need to get better at reacting faster and acting in a preventative way. our common foreign and security policy must be political, with clear priorities, and not crisis management as has often been the case in the past.
the rapporteur. - mr. speaker, i have to go. this is another one of those cases in the european parliament with a long and complicated name: community regime for the control of exports, transfer, brokering and transit of dual-use items. i don't think many people outside this room really understand what this means. but it is important because it is about the types of products that can be exported and under what circumstances, and it is about the types of products that can be used for civilian purposes to help people but can also be used for military purposes and to cause harm. if there is one thing i have learned in this process and one thing we have taken for granted, it is the need for more joint action in the european union. the crisis in ukraine and the complicated relations we have developed with russia are good examples of the need to act quickly and jointly. we export many products to russia, not least products that can be used for both peaceful and military purposes. another major challenge in this area is, of course, all the new products that can be used for civilian purposes as well as for military purposes. there are a lot of new technologies that can connect closed countries and open them up to the outside world, which is good for democracy, human rights and peace, but which can also be used for surveillance or for oppressing people. this type of product should also be covered by this type of legislation. here we have many substantial problems and challenges that require updating legislation, and the commission has promised that we will review the whole legislation on these dual-use products. this particular case is actually a practical case and a rather complicated technical case which originally concerned how we should adapt our legislation to the european parliament's codecision, so that we here in the house also have a say in how we should sell and export this type of product. we have also tried to speed up the process, which the crisis in ukraine has shown us is necessary. as rapporteur on this matter, i have found that the challenge has been the fact that we in the european parliament are not content to be told that there will be updates later and that all problems will soon be dealt with. we are impatient and wanted all the updates and modernisation of the regulatory framework to take place as soon as possible, and unfortunately we were stuck for quite some time in a conflict as to whether this was a technical matter and just an adaptation to the lisbon treaty or whether we were going to try to make all the necessary updates as soon as possible. i am glad to note, however, that we have found a compromise and a way forward with good will, and that we have managed to find a way of making a joint statement by the commission, the council of ministers and the european parliament committing ourselves to making the updates as soon as possible, and that we have also indicated what updates we think need to be made. the important thing is that we in the european parliament have a say in the matter. it is important that this be done quickly, but the most important thing of all is that we review the legislation. before that process, i want to send a clear signal to the commission: i think the commission should be bold and make sure that we get more harmonisation. i think it is unfortunate that different countries in europe take different paths and that different countries have different levels of tolerance for exporting what could be weapons to countries that perhaps should not have them. but i would like to thank everyone involved, the shadow rapporteurs, the commission, karel de grucht and also the presidency for their hard work in making this compromise happen.
member of the commission. - mr president, i do not have much to add. let me just confirm that we hope that by the end of this month we will come forward with a communication, and then, of course, that communication will be discussed with the next parliament. the commission will take initiatives on the basis of this debate, but that is, of course, also a matter for the next college.
rapporteur. - mr president, i would like to take this opportunity to thank commissioner de gucht, not only for the cooperation on this file, but also for the cooperation that i have had or rather – and i think i speak for the whole parliament – that we have had with him during this mandate. commissioner, this might be the last time, at least for me, that i personally have a debate with you in this respect. i know you like a good debate so it is therefore in one sense sad that we did not have more conflict than we had right now. but i think that, honestly, you can be proud of what you have achieved. during times of crisis when we need free trade the most, the appetite for free trade tends to be the smallest, so to speak. people do not like free trade in times of crisis but even though we have had one of the biggest crises ever in europe, you can take pride in actually having promoted free trade and advanced free trade both in europe and the rest of the world. therefore, when you leave office – in this capacity at least – you should remember the legacy you have left in promoting free trade, because i for one will also do so.

in writing. - i've put my vote down. andersdóttir's report is a good one and has many positive aspects. but since i am not convinced that the sst programme has only civilian applications, and since there is no detailed budget for the financing of the whole programme, i choose to abstain.
in writing. the climate crisis is the greatest challenge of our time. greenhouse gas emissions must be reduced while avoiding an increase in the costs of european industry. to do that, we need nuclear power. but more wind and other renewable energy sources will not be enough to cope with both climate and jobs. if nuclear power is phased out, europe may be forced to use dirty coal power from germany or gas from russia. energy policy is also security policy today. it is therefore not reasonable to propose strong limits on nuclear power, and i therefore chose to abstain in the final vote.

"we swedish social democrats believe it is very important to strengthen the fight against tax fraud, tax evasion and aggressive tax planning. a key part of that work is the european commission's proposal to close the loopholes in the parent/subsidiary directive that open up certain forms of aggressive tax planning. we are generally in favour of the proposal. that is why we voted in favour of the report which has now been put to the vote. at the same time, we note that there is a subsidiarity problem linked to the part of the proposal which is to establish a general anti-abuse provision in the directive. there are reasonable grounds for considering that such provisions are a matter for national jurisdiction. this aspect must be included in the continuing process.'
in writing. - i supported the resolution on the stockholm programme. i have been committed to the implementation of the stockholm programme, since its adoption during the swedish presidency. we took an historic step forward with the adoption of the asylum package to improve the conditions for those who come to europe while ensuring legal certainty and responsibility sharing. we improved the conditions for unaccompanied minors. we made border control more effective with a new system of borders surveillance, eurosur, which will help us to prevent tragedies like lampedusa, rescue people at sea and better manage external borders. we increased protection of women, with the european protection order and the directive on protection of victims; we adopted concrete tools to fight cross-border crime, with the european investigation order, the directive against human trafficking and the directive against child pornography. these are just a few examples of what we have accomplished in order to strike a real balance between providing security to citizens while respecting their rights and integrity. now it is time for member states to implement these tools to ensure that this vision of an open and secure europe, a europe of rights, for citizens, with citizens, becomes a reality.
