in writing. - for us social democrats, it is a matter of course that the aviation sector should contribute to the joint work to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. it is in line with eu decisions already taken and is based on the principle that those who cause environmental damage should share in the payment of the socioeconomic costs incurred. it is important for us to be able to reach a global system to reduce aviation emissions. that is why we supported the 'stop the clock' initiative, where other international actors promised to help find a solution. that's not what happened. we therefore believe that the eu should continue its own climate action on aviation, especially when it takes place in eu airspace. we think it is desirable that the revenue from the ets be allocated to climate action, but at the same time we defend the principle that member states should decide on the distribution of the revenue themselves.
in writing. - we swedish social democrats chose to abstain in the final vote on the two parts of the audit package. on the one hand, we welcome the fact that stronger legislation is in place. the problems in the industry are obvious and need to be fixed. there were relevant subsidiarity objections to the original proposal of the european commission, but these objections have been relatively largely addressed in the negotiation process. on the other hand, we believe that the current trialogue agreement has landed on the wrong side of the spectrum in key areas. rotation times of up to 20/24 years are not reasonable. nor is it the provision that the income from ancillary services may represent as much as 70% of the income from the audit activity itself. with this balanced picture, it made sense for us to abstain.
in writing. - i welcome the fact that the european parliament clearly took a position in favour of the trialogue agreement with the member states and the commission on the legislative package on auditing. but even though i voted for the compromise, i remain very sceptical about the rules on compulsory rotation of seats. this risked not leading to the increased competition that the commission wanted to achieve but rather strengthening the position of the four major players on the audit market. the office rotation also risks leading to an increase in costs of at least eur 10 billion for the eu as a whole. i have also said in the debates in the european parliament that there are not sufficient grounds for a regulation and that the rules should be adopted by means of a directive instead. a directive takes account of the way in which national audit markets operate and leaves room for national interpretation of the rules. unfortunately, the agreement became eu regulation in the form of both a directive and a regulation.
in writing. - i welcome the fact that the european parliament clearly took a position in favour of the trialogue agreement with the member states and the commission on the legislative package on auditing. but even though i voted for the compromise, i remain very sceptical about the rules on compulsory rotation of seats. this risked not leading to the increased competition that the commission wanted to achieve but rather strengthening the position of the four major players on the audit market. the office rotation also risks leading to an increase in costs of at least eur 10 billion for the eu as a whole. i have also said in the debates in the european parliament that there are not sufficient grounds for a regulation and that the rules should be adopted by means of a directive instead. a directive takes account of the way in which national audit markets operate and leaves room for national interpretation of the rules. unfortunately, the agreement became eu regulation in the form of both a directive and a regulation.
in writing. - the annual report of the european court of auditors showed an increase in the overall error rate in the implementation of the eu budget for the third year in a row. this is very troubling. the european commission is ultimately responsible for the use of eu money. however, the court's criticism is not directed primarily at the commission but at the member states. the main areas of failure are those eu policies which are subject to 'shared management' between the eu and the member states (i.e. areas of co-financing, especially the structural funds) and the member states' own management systems. the main spending areas of the eu budget - regional and agricultural policies - account for the largest share of the overall increase in errors. therefore, although the european commission bears the ultimate responsibility, we do not consider that there is any reason to deny the commission a full discharge for 2012. we therefore decided to abstain on this part of the report and would like to encourage the commission to initiate a discussion in the council between the member states in order to come up with measures to reduce the error rate. finally, we would also point out that the error rate estimated by the eu's auditors is usually not about waste or irregularities. in particular, these are pure errors on the part of the beneficiaries, such as incorrectly filled forms. in all cases where it is found that eu funds have been wrongly paid, the commission shall request that they be reimbursed.
- we would point out, however, that we do not support the wording of own resources in paragraph 12, as we do not consider it regrettable that own resources are no longer the main source of revenue for the eu budget, even though the protection of the eu's financial interests is very important precisely to ensure that citizens' money is used properly. however, this paragraph was not put to a separate vote.
in writing. - the fight against fraud is particularly important today, as the economic crisis is still being felt. according to commission estimates, some eur 600 million is lost each year through fraud and negligence. the eu's money must be used as efficiently as possible and then these enormous sums cannot disappear. we wholeheartedly support the fight against fraud with eu resources. the report also contains a general paragraph on the eu's own resources. the eu's own resources are important, but we do not want to see them extended to financial transaction taxes or other taxes. the fight against fraud is of the utmost importance, and we therefore voted in favour of the whole of the own-initiative report, even though we do not support the idea of extending the eu's own resources.
the rapporteur. i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals. a proposal was put forward called the animal health act, which would be based on the principle that prevention is better than cure. of course, this gave a tremendous boost to all those in europe who work with animals and animal welfare. you know very well that the welfare of animals is the detail on which most citizens write to the european parliament. this was a tremendous complexity when i started this work as rapporteur. this is not an animal health act, this is a disease control act. it is very important to distinguish between these. it means that so many people - and i fully understand why they have reacted this way - have thought that there is finally a proposal that will allow us to start creating better living conditions for our animals, whatever animals we are talking about. thank god i've had some amazing shadow speakers. we've done a good job together. i've been fortunate that they've been objective, knowledgeable and realistic. there has also been good cooperation with commission officials, efsa and veterinary and farmer organisations in europe. the really serious problem and challenge now that we realize that this is a disease control law is that we, unfortunately, have to be able to pick out all the animal care and animal welfare requirements that have been in this job. the great challenge for the european parliament is to take a decision which makes free trade in the internal market and the development of the european internal market in all its aspects a central point for getting the economy and employment going everywhere. but free trade in live animals or animal products must not mean free trade in serious diseases, even less serious diseases. i think that in our compromise we in the european parliament have found an excellent and lasting solution to the problem. the aim is that areas, member states or regions free of certain communicable diseases should be entitled to impose import restrictions if they are scientifically justified. they are obliged to inform the commission, and the commission can check that they are scientifically based. this is important, because a free market in this respect requires a well-functioning law on the control of infection.
the submission of an opinion by the committee on fisheries. - mr. speaker, i have to go. as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on fisheries on the new animal health law, i have taken as my starting point two important principles: firstly, prevention is better than cure and secondly, we must say no to free trade in animal diseases within the eu. we're not taking any unnecessary risks. areas and countries which are currently free of disease must have the right to protect themselves. trade in meat is one thing, but trade in live animals, whether fish or quadrupeds, is quite another, as it increases the risk of infection. every year, at least 25 000 people in the eu die from infections caused by antibiotic-resistant bacteria, almost as many as die in european traffic. we must take this very, very seriously. there are several eu directives on animal husbandry which are unfortunately not being complied with today. this means that the countries that follow the rules are discriminated against in the single market, where the rules are supposed to be the same for everyone. however, they are not, and that is why many swedish pig farmers are bankrupt today. i call on the commission to ensure that eu laws are respected. in doing so, we are actually preventing the spread of infection that this law is designed to prevent.
mr president, i would like to thank you. many of us have been working for a long time to introduce in the eu a maximum limit of eight hours for transporting animals for slaughter. it is really about animal health, and our patience is running out when it comes to waiting for proposals from the european commission. that is why i shall vote tomorrow for a maximum limit of eight hours for the transport of animals for slaughter. the focus should be on animal welfare requirements in general, and how they can be improved and better enforced within the eu. this is how we can prevent the diseases we see in animals and also how we can have a more level playing field within the eu. as far as public controls in the food chain are concerned, it is of course of the utmost importance that we have a strong regulatory framework in this area to protect human health - indeed, human life, if need be. it is good that attention is being paid to the problem of antibiotic resistance and that public controls in the future should be aimed at reducing antibiotic resistance. antibiotics have probably saved my life, and they have definitely reduced the suffering of my children. i hope that we can save more lives and reduce more suffering in the future with antibiotics, but that requires more action. i really hope that the next european commission will take action and put forward more concrete proposals on how we prevent antibiotic resistance in the eu.
the rapporteur. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. thank you for all the comments. as i said at the beginning, the term 'animal health law' clearly links animal welfare to matters outside this legislation. commissioner, if i am re-elected, i promise to fight to make this law extremely clear, because millions and millions of people in europe will follow it in their daily work. clearly, then, one of our most important duties is to make laws that are clear and understandable. as regards rapid action, there is nothing in the european parliament's proposal - which will be voted on tomorrow - that in any way prevents the commission from taking rapid and effective action for both known and as yet unknown diseases. what i would like to defend most strongly is the fight against antibiotic-resistant strains of bacteria. the countries and regions free of a disease must have the right to protect their freedom on a scientific, fact-based basis. a free market requires effective controls.
madam president, i would like to thank you. of course, we should have a mandatory register for lobbyists. but that is not enough, we must also support the green group's proposal to make it compulsory to ensure that the information lobbyists send to parliament is also made public. it is important that everyone knows where proposals come from in order to determine their impact. i really hope that the house will support this motion. that burden should not be on members of parliament, but the board should ensure that it is the responsibility of the lobbyists. it is regrettable that i have not been able to get support for my proposals on criminal law or at least fines and fees for those lobbyists who break the rules. just replacing one lobbyist with another is not a sufficient consequence to ensure that they comply with the regulations. we need to tighten up the lobbying rules. otherwise, it threatens the future development of the eu. lobbying today tends to preserve old power structures and old economic models.
rapporteur. - madam president, today we are to decide upon the basis for the european banking sector and banking union, with the banking recovery resolution directive being the cornerstone of that. on 11[nbsp ]december last year, we managed to reach a political agreement on what is essentially a fundamentally new way of legislation. until now, there have been no common rules on how to handle the failure of a bank either at european level or, in many cases, at national level. this new legislation stipulates what has to happen when a bank needs to be resolved – that is, wound down in an orderly fashion with the minimum impact on taxpayers and financial stability. it establishes the berlin principle, which means that banks’ shareholders and creditors will be the first to face the losses from a bank failure. it also lays down the framework for how the authorities of home and host member states are to deal with one another, clarifying the responsibilities of the different authorities. but it also states that, although berlin will be the main way to solve problems, there are also situations of systemic crisis when you need public intervention. in the future, no one should presume that taxpayers will help any bank, but we shall not preclude the need for public intervention in order to safeguard financial stability. i am happy that we have achieved the right balance for this year. the brrd, the banking resolution recovery directive, also lays the foundation of banking union and the forthcoming single resolution mechanism. in this regard, the point of departure will be that rules are set for all 28 member states and we will have the same rules in all of the union. i think that this is fundamentally important, because it means that when there are discussions in the eurozone about banking union, we will not have the risk of a fragmented union for banks, because we have the same platform, with the recovery resolution directive as its cornerstone. i think this is an essential thing to think about in the future, because the strength of the european economy and european financial markets is their ability to act in a unified way and mobilise all the resources we have in our different markets, making them into one financial market. looking at the wider horizon, we are now facing the votes we will be casting later today on this and other dossiers, which mark not only the end of this mandate but also an era of regulation of financial markets probably never seen before. i think it is worthwhile to use the coming years for a time of reflection: how far did we go, how successful were we and what will be the results when we implement all this legislation? i think it is important to see how the european financial markets work after implementation of these enormous, ambitious regulations. i think it is fair to say that we are now laying the ground for a stable banking sector, but also with opportunities for dynamic decisions, because we are underlining the need for responsibilities in the management of each bank. when banks and bank owners can no longer trust and rely on the feeling that someone else will save them, the need for increased responsibility will be realised and we will see that, in that sense, we will have regained the principles of market economy in the banking sector. we have done a lot: we have higher capital levels, harmonised rules for deposit guarantee schemes, and we will have new rules for markets and financial instruments, otc derivatives, etc., and i think when we take an overall view of all this, we will see that we have achieved a lot and now it is time to see how it works. i think we could also take a look at some of the other things that we will need. we will need a dynamic financial market and i think the litmus test will be whether we can get the european economy to grow. i am proud of the result of our work. i would like to express my gratitude to my colleagues for their very professional cooperation and good spirits during this procedure. thank you, elisa, wolf, philippe, vicky and all of your staff; thank you to the secretariat of the committee on economic and monetary affairs for invaluable effort and expertise and thank you, commissioner barnier, for all the work you and your staff have done. we have prepared the ground for a more stable and more dynamic european banking sector. now let us wait for growth and new jobs to come.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the banking union is perhaps the most important vote of the entire election cycle. we now have a single, strong european supervisory authority in the form of the ecb, a single stability fund to avoid taxpayers being affected and a deposit guarantee scheme. monetary union has been questioned by economists and opinion makers since its inception. those who did not believe that the euro would become a reality were wrong. those who thought the euro would collapse were wrong. those who thought two years ago that greece would be forced to leave the monetary union were also wrong. perhaps it is time for reflection for those who did not understand the decisiveness that the union can provide in times of unrest. europe is emerging from the worst crisis since the 1920s and 1930s. mr. deputy prime minister, greece has returned to the international bond market. my congratulations! ireland and portugal are also recovering. the eu has shown its strength in difficult times. the banking union creates stability and security, but reforms must now continue. madam president, i regret that sweden is outside the banking union. it is not good for the big swedish banks and their customers, and it weakens sweden's influence in the european union. this is one of my last speeches. i would like to take this opportunity to thank the president, the commissioner and the deputy prime minister. i think you have done a good job, and we can hope that my country will also join the monetary union eventually.
member of the commission. - madam president, it's a very long way we've come together, at twenty-eight i say this in relation to the remarks on the city of london. this path of 28 is not a matter of chance. it was a voluntary work on my part, on yours, and on the part of the governments, to work together for the unity of the internal market, including this very large financial centre, the city. it's the main one and it's a chance for all of us. this long journey began, ladies and gentlemen, with structural reforms and i say this in response to the regret that we have arrived too late at the banking union. i would remind you that my priority was the creation of the three european supervisory authorities following on from the larosière report: the eba, the eiopa and the esma. the rapporteur was sylvie goulard, the rapporteur was sven giegold, the rapporteur was peter skinner and the rapporteur was your former colleague, mr garcía-margallo. all those who want to join the banking union are welcome, including sweden and the other countries, because it is clear that the stability of the eurozone or its instability, as we have seen for five years is the stability or instability of the entire european union. this banking union is therefore a fundamental element. elisa ferreira, marianne thyssen and sven giegold have worked a lot on this. but i want to, as corien wortmann-kool said, welcome the special role, alongside the greek presidency, of the president of the eurogroup, jeroen dijsselbloem, and - if you will allow me - also underline the major role of the european central bank in all this negotiation, with president draghi and vice-president vitor constâncio. structure, again, with the common rule, to avoid citizens or taxpayers paying for the banks; with the rules of the ebrd, as gunnar hökmark has just mentioned; with prudential rules, structural, for all the "insurance" players with the solvency regime. burkhard balz talked about it earlier and then othmar karas, who played a major role in the prudential rules for the 8 300 banks in the european union. these are structural reforms to reduce risk, ensure good supervision and accountability. the first key to accountability is transparency: we know who is doing what. and we've shed light on a number of people who don't like the light very much. we'll have to keep going. mairead mcguinness, i'd like to use the words "morality" and "ethics" again. it is not unusual to speak of these words and to put behind them acts, laws, which are in the public interest. since i am talking about citizens, ethics, morality and also risks, i would like to reply to othmar karas on the specific point he raised, namely the technical standards on remuneration in the context of the crd. i welcome your agreement a few weeks ago on the technical standards for remuneration to define clearly what are called risk takers, to whom the crd rules on remuneration will apply. following my discussions in the econ committee, mr karas, i am well aware [nbsp] and i support the parliament's concern[nbsp] that the authorities must be informed ex ante of the application of these rules. this is precisely the way we interpret technical standards. i will confirm this interpretation, mr. karas, by a formal letter. this is a situation where the citizens were waiting for answers and where you, as members of parliament, have given answers on the basis of work with the commission and the council, so that the financial world does not think that everything is permitted, that everything is not allowed, as has been the case for twenty-five or thirty years. this world of finance, as mr gauss said, must return to the service of the real economy rather than to its own service. we have worked a lot and since i am talking about the citizens and taxpayers whom we wanted to protect, i would also like to mention some of the texts we have adopted over the last four years, which are precisely citizens' texts: the deposit guarantee is a citizen's text, mr simon; the prips, mrs beres; the basic bank account, mr jürgen klute, and i also welcome the presence of my colleague neven mimica, who followed this text very closely; the antolín sánchez presedo mortgage credit; the guarantee and compensation of investors with olle schmidt; the framework for payment services and interchange fees, with pablo zalba and diogo feio. all these citizen texts concern the daily lives of individuals and families who, by the thousands, have suffered from the lack of transparency, sometimes manipulation and lack of accountability. we protected the citizens. madam president, it is also worth taking a minute or two to recall the work done by all the actors and sectors, from the text on alternative funds and private equity reported by jean-paul gauzès to mr el khadraoui's shadow banking and market funds and the text on derivatives. werner langen has spoken, and i thank him for his appreciation. he has done a lot of work on this sector, which represents, in the world of exchanges, some 600,000 billion euros, of which 80% is over-the-counter, not transparent. we have shed light and we will have to go further and continue: short selling, with philippe lamberts; financial markets [nbsp]markus ferber, just now mentioned this central text ; market abuse; control of manipulations [nbsp]some bankers will go to prison now for the most serious manipulations , this is the work of arlene mccarthy; the rating agencies with mr. domenici; the ucits with mr. sven giegold; the audit with mr. sajjad karim; the securities with ms. swinburne; the simplifications of accounting with mr. lehne, whom we welcome beyond this house; the financial conglomerates with mr. stolojan. i will mention two other texts which are important to me in the context of the internal market because they are also texts which facilitate growth: the passport on venture capital, which mr lamberts and mrs hübner were responsible for, and the european social entrepreneurship fund, which is a sector which is currently creating jobs on the ground, for which mrs sophie auconie was rapporteur. i wanted to mention all these rapporteurs because they, their collaborators, the commission services, the interpreters - i salute them - and the legal service have done a considerable amount of work, to which we have also contributed. this is not over, ladies and gentlemen. many of you, i hope, will come back here to this house to continue the work on longterm finance, for which mrs rodi [nbsp ]kratsa is rapporteur, on benchmarks, which chair sharon bowles has dealt with, on non-financial reporting, with mr baldassarre. as regards the structural reform of the banks, ladies and gentlemen, i know that the text has come late. i wanted, logically, to do all the rest, that is to say all the horizontal cross-cutting texts that concern the 8 300 banks: resolution, deposit guarantee, supervision, capitalisation, robustness. and it was after i had drawn up all these texts and after they had been adopted, after they had been specified in detail, that i asked myself the question: is this enough? and i said, "yes, it is enough, if these texts are adopted by almost all banks and if they are properly observed under the authority of the supervisors". my answer is 'no' to the 30 largest european banks, whose balance sheets are often larger than the gdp of the country in which they are located. and if one of them has a problem, it is not just the question of the country in which its head office is located, it is a systemic problem for the whole european union. that is why i have proposed this last structural reform, which your assembly will have to adopt after its renewal. as many of you will be returning to parliament, if the citizens want it, you will have to carry this reform to heart. in any case, wherever i am, i will follow it very closely because i believe it is very important to separate the weighting and risk management and perhaps also to prohibit the most speculative activities, what is called property trading, which has no justification from the point of view of the general interest and the economy for the largest banks. ladies and gentlemen, we must ensure that these texts are properly applied, we must evaluate them, supplement them and correct them if necessary. we also need to ensure a level playing field with all our partners. i would remind you that it is the european union which is behind the g20 and the whole agenda of global regulations to learn from the crisis and to prevent it from happening again. we have done our job and we need to check with our partners, the americans in particular, but also the other g20 partners, that everyone is doing their job. the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: this is the work i did! some bankers think it's a lot. i think what has done a lot is the lack of regulation. that's the self-regulation i don't believe in. it was deregulation, which europe has sometimes supported for 30 years, that caused this financial crisis. fair rules, good supervision and public regulation were needed. we have done it together and i would point out, madam president, that all the political groups in this house have participated in one way or another in it through the rapporteurs i have mentioned. this is not the time for complacency, there are too many european citizens who are suffering, who have efforts to make, who have been asked to make a lot of effort. let us think of them on the basis of what i have indicated, of stabilized financial markets and better rules. we must now focus all our efforts on recovery, growth and competitiveness.
he is seeking the presidency of the council. - i know . mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as i happened to be the finance minister in greece at the most difficult moment of the financial crisis, when the financial crisis turned into a banking crisis, because of the need to drastically reduce greek public debt, i can, thanks to my experience and experience, assure you that what is happening today, this decision you are going to take on the banking union and the single resolution mechanism, is a very important act and, i dare say, a historic act, because we will break the tie that exists and allow the possible fiscal crisis to turn into a financial and financial crisis and vice versa. this is a vicious cycle we must not repeat. the attempt to fill the fundamental and inherent gaps of economic and monetary union is difficult. the completion of economic and monetary union requires a great deal of work. i agree with the honourable members who have stressed earlier that it is not enough to have a single supervisory mechanism and it is not enough to have a single resolution mechanism, with all the problems and imperfections it has, but we also need a truly single guarantee protection mechanism at pan-european level which goes beyond the objectives of the directive, which are the objectives of harmonising national guarantee systems. to a large extent, what is happening is an attempt to overcome very significant internal disparities between member states and structural disparities in the single market in the financial sector. the most important inequality in the european banking market, in my view, is the inequality of interest rates, because it reproduces profound developmental disparities between member states. when a greek small or medium-sized enterprise borrows money from banks at an interest rate 6 or 7 per cent higher than the interest rate at which a german enterprise borrows, then an internal development and social inequality is reproduced. this is due in large part to the fact that there is no banking union. a very significant inequality that we continue to face in greece and other countries is the withdrawal of deposits to be transferred to another country, not because there is a better interest rate - the interest rate may be lower - but because there is a deeper sense of security; something that is very, very important. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's financial system is not undermined by the financial crisis. but now, a few days before the european elections, a very important question arises, now that we must all formulate our comprehensive proposal for the future of european integration as we address citizens and especially young european citizens who are unemployed. the question is why they should be interested in what is happening in the banking union and why it is important for them to have a single supervisory mechanism and a single deposit-reconciliation mechanism. we must explain to the citizens that these are preconditions for supporting the european real economy in a more healthy and competitive way and for supporting european businesses on equal terms, in a healthy way, at rates which are really competitive. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the conclusion is therefore that the banking union is not only about banks, bankers, hybrid products, shareholders, financial games, that is, it is not only limited to the financial sphere but it is ultimately and above all about the real economy, where europe is suffering and where the agony of every european citizen is expressed, especially those who are unemployed or, marginally, living in poverty. it is therefore very important that we talk about all that needs to be done. i agree with commissioner barnier, whom i also honour and commend for his work, his persistence, his firm orientation when he says that we must take further steps. i would like to point out that the complete separation of investment and commercial banks is a very important step and an element of transparency. it is of the utmost importance that we tell the truth about our objectives in the banking union. obviously we want to check the quality of the portfolios of banks, both investment and commercial. we obviously want to check the criteria and the quality of the loans from the banks. these checks, through the single supervisory mechanism, ultimately help growth, help healthy competitiveness, help investment and help, finally, to tackle the great problem of unemployment. there is a thought that behind all this is the fact that the financial sphere is completely separated from the real economy and is self-sustaining, that it easily makes money through financial games and that this is a story without end, that we can thus move into the future, without any risk, accumulating surpluses. this is getting us nowhere. turns out it leads nowhere. i should also say that the quality control of the portfolios of the 130 european systemic banks ultimately leads to an indirect assessment of government bonds and therefore brings us face to face with the issue of european public debt, because of course the management of european public debt, the public debt of the member states, cannot be done by the states or the european central bank or the european stability mechanism alone. we need the european banking system, but we need that european banking system which ensures, through participation in the market for government bonds in europe, the acquisition and maintenance of a healthy portfolio which, in turn, will help the real economies of the member states. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. it is therefore very important that we address the european citizens by talking to them about things that concern them, explaining to them that what we are doing is not abstract concepts and does not concern a closed world of financial transactions but concerns the real europe, the european societies, the peoples and citizens. thank you very much.
the rapporteur. - madam speaker, please. i would like to thank commissioner michel barnier and his colleagues, minister evangelos venizelos, the greek presidency and also the former lithuanian presidency, which made it possible to conclude the political agreement between parliament and the council. we are now at the end of a period of intensive regulation, in which we are laying the foundations for european banks and for future european banking regulation. we have laid the foundations for clear rules on public finances in the past. if there is no balance in public finances, there is no stability in the economy. these two things are clearly linked. what we are doing now is we are entering a new period where this has to be done, where we have to ensure that what we have done in terms of stability also contributes to the growth, jobs and investment that is needed. what we're doing now is laying the foundation for banks that can be more stable. through the berlin process we have, we are not only forcing forward the stability of each individual bank but also the stability of the system when the crisis comes. we are giving banks an opportunity to be more european by solving the problems that have existed between host and home countries but also ensuring that we have common legislation in all 28 member states. in addition, we are making the banks more credible, which will facilitate their ability to finance the investment and growth that europe needs. it will be clear that no one can trust the government or the taxpayers to solve the problems of the banks. bank owners and lenders now know that they are taking the same risks as in any other business. we ensure that the common public ultimately always ensures the stability of the system. now there's a chance to move on. i would like to thank everyone who has been involved in this debate here today, because there is broad support for implementing these fundamental measures. the time has come to ensure that they are implemented in such a way that we can create the jobs, growth and investment that europe needs. we have laid that foundation, and i am proud to have been a part of it.
mr president, i would like to thank you. consumer products must be safe and of high quality. but quality is determined by testing, not by the country of manufacture. i am against origin labelling and was also a shadow rapporteur on the customs code, the union customs code, but i had to give in and accept a compromise as heide rühle mentioned earlier. however, protectionist labelling - that is what we are talking about here - should not be included in this regulation. the eu rules on origin labelling are complex and will increase the cost of handling consumer products. a cost which will ultimately be borne by eu consumers. europe's businesses, especially small businesses, do not need more bureaucracy and increased costs. on the contrary, you need less bureaucracy and less costs. (the speaker agreed to answer a question (blue card) in accordance with rule 149 (8) of the rules of procedure).
blue-card answer. – i can agree with a lot of the problems you mention here, but it has nothing to do with what i am proposing. of course we differ, as i am a free trader: i think free trade is very good. i know that this labelling is used to protect european goods and european industry in a false manner. if you want to get rid of all these problems you mention, this is not the right way. it will increase costs and will be less competitive for the european market and for the smes that you believe that you are protecting. free trade is actually helping europe, not protectionism.
in writing. - it is important for the stability of europe's banking sector that a common banking union and a common crisis fund can be established, and we believe that sweden should be part of this union in the long term. we therefore voted in favour of the tripartite agreement on banking union. we want to emphasise that we are against using the eu budget or national budgets to rescue banks in crisis. swedish or other national taxpayers will not be responsible for losses in foreign banks if we join the banking union. each eu country has a responsibility to manage its own public finances, as does the banks' crisis fund, which is to be financed by the banks themselves. rather than draining sovereign finances or increasing speculation, a crisis fund of banks' own money should encourage long-term thinking and enable a more stable financial market. for these reasons we voted in favour of the report.
in writing. - the financial instability created by the economic crisis was partly due to the different deposit guarantee systems in the member states. this created uncertainty and a shifting environment for financial operators. many countries also finance their deposit guarantee schemes directly from their budget without buffered funds. this must end in order to ensure the liquidity of the banking sector and to increase consumer and investor confidence. i support this tripartite agreement, which harmonises national rules on deposit guarantee within the eu and creates greater security for financial operators. the requirements for better information and faster payouts to depositors improve market confidence. since the agreement also preserves the deposit guarantee of eur 100000 per person, all bank customers receive an equivalent guarantee from all european banks.
in writing. - during the financial crisis, it became clear that many banks and credit institutions lacked effective tools to deal with crisis situations. to remedy this and avoid similar crises, banks need to be able to finance their own crisis management. public funds shall not be used in state rescue operations. i believe that all banks should have recovery and restructuring plans in place, so-called living wills for how to proceed in the event of an orderly bankruptcy. these ensure that banks are not caught by surprise by any crisis. this will increase the capacity of banks to act in times of turmoil and avoid a deeper crisis. a more robust banking sector benefits everyone, and that is why i voted in favour of this proposal.
in writing. during the financial crisis, individuals like bernard madoff showed that the existing rules for financial institutions were inadequate. his and others' pyramid-style investment fund fraud damaged confidence in these products and therefore the regulation of this market needs to be tightened. clear and concise financial market regulation benefits all market participants and can prevent future fraud. i therefore support this proposal for clearer rules for depositary institutions and their stock and interest-bearing funds. i also welcome the tightening of supervision of depositaries by the tripartite agreement. it is important that financial market reforms are now being implemented and that infringements of these reforms are punished. the proposed tripartite agreement contains more detailed rules on sanctions and increased protection for whistleblowers. this will effectively prevent and detect overruns, while increasing market transparency. i voted for the motion for these reasons.
in writing. - today, some 58 million europeans do not have a regular bank account. not having a bank account today is like being an outsider. you can't get your salary, pension or other benefits into an account, a system that's also exploited by the black economy. this is not only discriminatory but also a real obstacle to free movement within the eu. banks may not refuse to open a bank account for a consumer on the grounds that he has a foreign social security number. all that is required is that you can prove your identity, which is a reasonable requirement to prevent money laundering. i have long called for affordable financial services for those who are not attractive bank customers, and i am pleased today to vote in favour of the proposal.
in writing. - following the financial crisis in europe, it is important to improve transparency, clarity and knowledge in financial markets. if consumers are well informed, the market functions better, while maintaining confidence in the financial market. i therefore welcome this tripartite agreement on the introduction of the fund fact sheet for packaged investment products. common rules for those investment products improve comparability and comprehensibility for the investor while creating a level playing field in the internal market. the tripartite agreement covers a wide range of financial products and therefore ensures that the investor can be sure of what an investment entails. an effective system of penalties ensures that dealers who provide incorrect or misleading information are punished, thereby preventing future infringements. for these reasons, i support this agreement.
in writing. - it would be great if more citizens could have access to high-speed broadband. it is a development that is necessary. but it is hardly something that can be managed from within the eu, and the details of how this should be designed have to be decided at national and local level. i therefore chose to abstain in the final vote.
in writing. - i voted against the agreement on information and promotion measures for agricultural products on the internal market and in third countries, because i do not think it is reasonable to spend eur 883 million on this. these are resources that could be better used elsewhere.
in the light of the recent developments in the field of the common agricultural policy, the commission has decided to propose a new regulation on the common organization of the market in cereals, the common organization of the market in milk and milk products, the common organization of the market in milk and milk products, and the common organization of the market in milk and milk products.
in writing. - i supported the directive to facilitate the admission of highly skilled professionals from third countries coming to europe as intra-corporate transferees (icts). geographical boundaries should not hinder business growth in a knowledge-based economy. with common and clear rules it will be easier for international companies to transfer employees temporarily to the eu and european companies will get faster and easier access to skilled workers from around the world. this is particularly important for sweden, where innovation and creativity are so important to attracting investments and creating jobs. i am particularly happy that we introduced the possibility for intra-corporate transferees to move and work for the same company in another member state without initiating a new procedure. we have also introduced safeguards to ensure that working conditions and equal treatment are respected. this directive will contribute to sweden’s competitiveness and economic growth and i will continue to be committed in the next mandate to ensuring that all member states will implement the new rules.
in writing. - we agree with the writings that the eu budget must be spent on the right things and provide european added value. we also agree with the criticism that in several cases the council did not respect the right of co-decision of the parliament and instead negotiated internally on issues that should have been negotiated jointly with the european parliament, such as several aspects of eu cohesion policy. however, the report also proposes, in our view, too far-reaching changes in the way the eu budget is financed and adopted. we do not believe that more 'real' own resources should be introduced and we would like to stress the importance of sweden's continued veto in the negotiations on a new long-term budget.
madam president, i would like to thank you. freedom of movement shall not be restricted. it is a great asset and one of the best things the eu has done. it is certainly a good thing when people can move between member states to do work that is needed in one country after another. it is a win-win for the individual who gains increased job opportunities and for the countries that benefit from the skills of the seconded person. now we hear voices raised to tear up the directive. it would be a failure indeed, because we are part of our surroundings and part of the european context. we cannot isolate ourselves in a globalized world. to set criteria and requirements which would make it difficult for the dynamic labour market to function is pure and simple protectionism. i am surprised that these demands come from both social democrats and leftists. instead, it is our national systems that should be updated in line with a dynamic world. today, about one million eu citizens are posted to work in another member state. the directive sets out core rights such as minimum wages, holidays, health, working hours, etc. there are problems, of course, with pay and holidays, for example. that is why we should adopt the directive now and improve its implementation and protect workers. where there are decentralised collective agreements, we must also ensure that we can improve information, for example in sweden. we welcome that. mr president, i would like to thank you. free movement is perhaps one of the most important aspects of eu cooperation. freedom of movement to where the jobs are is a matter of course in an open and borderless europe. only 0.1% move between eu countries to look for work. within the united states, the same figure is 3 percent. in my home country, sweden, there are about 6,000 people who are posted out of the 5 million who work. we need even more crossborder mobility, not less. eu rules on posting must not restrict labour market mobility. as has already been said here, we in the alde group stand behind the tripartite compromise. common eu rules are needed to guarantee the social rights of posted workers, such as minimum wages, holidays and protection against discrimination. we call it the hard core. the left, on the other hand -- we hear it here -- wants to go further, which would limit mobility substantially. this, in my view, suggests protectionism and nationalism, and i do not think we need that in europe these days. we must of course - and i agree with olle ludvigsson - do more to tackle fraud and abuse of the rules and do more preventive work. but to scrap the posting directive, as the left is demanding, would be to go backwards and limit mobility to the detriment of workers, wage earners and europe.
madam president, i would like to thank you. for weeks my son had the habit of picking up trash from the ditch when we went to school. it's just that every day we walked in, there was new junk. it wasn't kids throwing it, because it was junk boxes, it was big bags of chips and it was system company boxes, but it was grown-up people doing it. i think it's terrible that people can't take responsibility for taking care of their own garbage, but they obviously can't. that is why it is really necessary for us to take a common political responsibility here in the eu and do something about the fact that our seas are now full of garbage, not only of visible plastic bags but also of this invisible plastic, that is to say plastic bags and other things that have already been broken down into very small pieces and that completely fill our oceans, that are eaten by animals and that end up on our plates. no, it simply requires that we do something about it. so it makes perfect sense to set reduction targets and of course to charge for plastic bags in the store. however, i cannot wholeheartedly support this proposal, because i see a risk in how it will work in my own country. there, we have a tradition of paying for a plastic bag in the grocery store that we use to throw out trash. it's thinner than what this proposal counts as reusable. the risk is therefore that it will be made thicker in the future in order to escape the reduction requirement. i hope that in the coming negotiations, the best will be done to clarify this, so that the legislation we finally get also produces a good result for the environment throughout the eu, including in my own country.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. almost every day we hear of another boat sinking in the mediterranean and more migrants drowning. of course, this culminated in the tragic accident off lampedusa a few months ago. the bitter truth is that the mediterranean, mare nostrum, is turning into a sea of death. the biggest problem, of course, is that there are no legal safe routes for refugees to come to the eu today. instead, they are forced into the hands of smugglers, putting their lives on the line in life-threatening boats to seek refuge in the eu. now it's time we establish legal safe routes for them to come here. at the same time, we must become better at handling situations at sea. until now, there have been no clear rules. i therefore warmly welcome the report on frontex's maritime operations. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr carlos coelho, from the bottom of my heart for his work. i would say that without his efforts this report would look very different. for the first time, we now have binding rules for search and rescue operations at sea. we are introducing mandatory measures to identify people and ensuring that those who need it receive special protection, both asylum seekers and vulnerable people. we are prohibiting pushbacks in international waters and are strengthening the text on solidarity and the principle of non-refoulement. i'm glad the work is far, far better than i dared hope when we started. i have complained at times, and i have been discouraged at times, but i am now calling on the member states to introduce similar rules for national operations. this is how we can make a difference and prevent the mediterranean from becoming a sea of death.

in the light of the recent developments in the fisheries sector, the commission has decided to extend the scope of the proposal to include the fisheries sector in the new regulation. the vote was on accepting the first reading agreed with the council in january 2014. the whole reform of the common fisheries policy was a revolutionary success, which means, among other things, that overfishing is now prohibited. the european maritime and fisheries fund that we voted on today was intended to support this reform towards sustainable fishing. unfortunately, many elements of the commission's legislative proposal deteriorated during the negotiations with parliament and the council. after intensive work we managed to remove the worst weaknesses, such as subsidies for new shipbuilding and subsidies for taking over someone else's fishing activities. we also secured more money for fisheries control and the collection of scientific information. despite these successes, we did not make it all the way. too many subsidies driving continued overfishing were included in the final settlement, including start-up subsidies for young fishermen, engine replacement subsidies and fish storage subsidies. that is why i voted against the report.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the court's opinion on the data retention directive is clear, almost written in fire. the legislation adopted in the member states must therefore now be reviewed immediately, so that any retention of data is only within the limits of what is allowed under the charter of rights. the directive is invalidated by the court of justice because storage in its present form is incompatible with our fundamental rights and freedoms. i think it's good that the court is so clear. it leaves no doubt as to what we must do now. we are demanding that the member states guarantee the rights and freedoms of their citizens. of course, eu legislation must also do so. the judgment now gives us a new opportunity to improve legislation in this area. i am saying that the collection of electronic data should be linked to crime and not done unless there is a suspicion of crime. it is also important that we now review the national implementing legislation and that member states take account of the court's conclusions in their national legislation. the resolution also raises the question of whether we should legislate directly through eu directives or through eu regulations. a regulation becomes law in the member states, whereas a directive is transposed into national law. the procedure is very complicated and it is difficult to ensure that it has been carried out correctly. that is why we should now focus more on working with regulations rather than directives. i particularly welcome the fact that the commission will today reimburse sweden the amount of the fine that sweden was forced to pay because we were not considered to have implemented the directive in time. and with that joy and that gratitude, i conclude my speech.

in writing. - we social democrats wholeheartedly support the objective of reducing the amount of plastic waste in the environment. unfortunately, a major problem in europe today is that plastic bags are largely released into the environment instead of being properly managed. the discard of plastic carrier bags in the wild is a major problem in europe and the rest of the world. we see its harmful effects first and foremost in our shared marine environment, where plastic waste has a huge negative impact on wildlife. we therefore welcome the commission's proposal to reduce the amount of plastic bags and to move towards more environmentally friendly alternatives. the consumption of plastic carrier bags varies across eu countries as a result of consumption patterns, national legislation and economic policies. this means that the conditions vary from one market to another. we have reservations about the european parliament's report being clumsy in this respect, because the thickness of the boxes is not the only factor determining whether or not the boxes are reused. the proposal as it stands now risks not reducing the use of plastic bags in swedish grocery stores but instead making them thicker. of course, this would not be good. despite these objections, we would still like to support the proposal at this first reading because it is important to reduce the use of plastic bags. we hope and are working to ensure that the forthcoming negotiations with the council of ministers will result in an ambitious regulatory framework which also takes account of swedish conditions. in addition, of course, there is a heavy responsibility on the swedish government to implement the forthcoming directive in a way that results in the greatest possible environmental benefit.

president of the council. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the council considers that the directive we are talking about, the 1992 directive on pregnant workers, is an integral part of the legislation on safety and health at work and we also believe that it is a very important directive which certainly needs to be updated in several respects. that is why the council has made it one of its priorities since 2008, when the european commission presented its proposal. the european parliament, too, has done a very important job, given the importance of the subject. the negotiations in the council were important negotiations, and they were also very difficult. it is very difficult because of the distance between the positions within the council and the considerable distance between the positions of the council of ministers and the european parliament. what are the main points of difficulty? substantial differences have emerged between the different national social systems in this area. these are very substantial differences and reflect a considerable diversity between the social systems at national level in the various member states. cost-related difficulties have emerged, difficulties which should not be underestimated in general terms but which should not be underestimated in particular at a time of considerable pressure on public budgets. finally, difficulties arose in the area of subsidiarity because of the very different traditions and practices of the various member states. as regards the differences in substance, i will give only as an example the question of the duration and timing of compulsory leave, on which there are strong differences and also strong differences of views between the various member states within the council. as far as costs are concerned, there has certainly been no agreement and i think it would be very difficult to negotiate a minimum of 20 weeks' leave as requested by the european parliament. this is a request on which there has been no advanced and thorough discussion and certainly no agreement in the council. i shall give you some other examples of subsidiarity. some member states have raised the fact that some of the rules covered by the legislative proposal are already included in national rules. i am thinking of breastfeeding time off, special working arrangements for parents of disabled children or supplementary maternity leave in certain cases. the council considered that the detailed provisions, including the detailed rules for implementing the minimum standards, were therefore a matter for the member states. more generally, however, member states have reiterated their commitment to the safety and health of pregnant workers and those who have given birth or are breastfeeding, and there is strong recognition within the council of the importance of reconciling work and private life. on the question of improving the existing rules on the protection of pregnant workers and promoting a better work-life balance, however, we must find the appropriate instruments in the right policy area. it would be difficult for the council to accept such proposals from the european parliament in the context of health and safety legislation. however, this in no way diminishes the valuable contribution which this parliament has made to the debate on this subject. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the discussions which i have just summarized in this speech have therefore revealed very concrete and substantial differences between the views of the council and the position of the european parliament at first reading, and it is in the light of these strong contradictions and strong differences that the commission has concluded that it must consider withdrawing its proposal. this is certainly a prerogative which the treaties give to the commission. the commission certainly has the possibility at any time to table and withdraw its legislative proposals. so these are institutional assessments which are the commission's responsibility and which only the commission can make. these are assessments of the proper functioning of the legislative process and the implementation of the refit rules, which are also assessed by the european commission. and then there are assessments of policy opportunities, and these, in particular, i would like to talk to you about in conclusion. we believe as the presidency in office that the issue of the protection of pregnant workers at european level is of the utmost importance, is of the utmost importance in the light of the wider social dimension that we must develop in the internal market and is also of the utmost importance as a matter of social rights. so we can try again with the rotating presidency, it is worth trying again, and we are also prepared, if we start from a new political base and a new beginning, to examine the proposal again in the council and in parliament. we are willing to see if the conditions are right, working with the new rapporteur, to restart, to try to prevent the proposal from coming to a dead end. it is clear that if we want to make progress and really want to reopen the subject, progress must be made on both sides. so we must certainly see whether the political conditions are there, starting with the relations between parliament and the council, to restart the legislative negotiations by listening to each other's arguments. the presidency is prepared, if the european parliament is equally prepared to make a new start on a different political basis, to consider whether this debate can be reopened in the council of ministers, given the importance we attach to this dossier, but without concealing all the difficulties and obstacles i have pointed out with examples in my introduction.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, the commission is committed to helping eu citizens, men and women, to better combine work and family life. the fact that the maternity leave proposal has been identified in the refit communication of june 2014 as a candidate for withdrawal does not change that. since 2008, when the proposal was presented, the commission has sought its adoption. the commission has acted as an honest broker between parliament and the council to find common ground. this has proven to be difficult. the commission’s proposal on the maternity leave directive has not been discussed for more than two and a half years despite numerous attempts by the commission to reinvigorate negotiations between co-legislators. the proposal has been effectively blocked in the legislative process for too long, with no progress at all for almost three years. in spite of considerable efforts it has been impossible to break this stalemate and engage in any serious negotiations. as you know, one of the intentions of the refit exercise is to withdraw proposals where no negotiations have taken place for a significant period of time and which lack any prospects for adoption. it does not mean that the commission does not believe anymore in its initial objectives. on the contrary it could allow for a fresh, new start on the subject to the greater benefit of eu citizens with a more modern text that would notably include in its scope paternity leave, as requested by parliament. the commission remains committed through its europe 2020 strategy to helping women to stay in the labour market rather than being excluded from it because they chose to have children. in this respect it issued country specific recommendations to several member states to stress the importance of creating the conditions for reconciliation of private and professional life.
for the ppe group. president. - the debate is closed. women and men should have the same right and opportunity to work. having children should not be a barrier to women in the workforce. stronger protection for working parents is needed, but the solution is not to legislate from brussels. i therefore welcome the commission's withdrawal of the maternity leave directive. family policy should be decided at national level. there are great differences in europe and i understand the challenges in some countries, but we cannot have a solution that suits everyone in the eu. in sweden, for example, we have come a long way. of the total 1.5 years per child, at least 2 months should be taken out of each parent, and the mother and father can get a bonus if they share more equally. the directive would therefore represent a major step backwards for the whole of the nordic region. it is incomprehensible that the swedish social democrats, who are sceptical of the eu, are now pushing to force swedish women to stay at home - whatever they want. the implementation of a ban on women working in childbirth in brussels is not the way forward for gender equality. maternity leave, madam president, is to be parental leave. the proposal sends the wrong signal by mentioning only women as parents and thus solely responsible for the children. it is shared parental leave that is true equality. we will encourage both parents to care for their children and spend time with them. it's a decision that should be made by the parents themselves and not dictated by politicians. of course, women should not have to give up their career ambitions, and it is important that employers give women the opportunity to return to the workforce without discrimination. it's about changing attitudes and fighting stereotypes. i would like to conclude, madam president, by saying that the ppe group will continue to be committed to ensuring that women who give birth are not discriminated against in working life. madam president, this is my first time speaking in this political house and that is why i want to start by making certain things clear. i have already said this to mr juncker at a meeting when he visited our group and met members who are fighting for sovereignty and democracy in the efdd. we love europe, which is a beautiful continent, but we are against this terrible union. we really want to work together with other countries in europe, of course, but not in this way, and we do not want to lose our own sovereignty. every day we see national powers being transferred to brussels; it will not take long before we have no say any more about our own future. the united states of europe will be a fact, with sweden as a province. in sweden we have 25% of young people out of work. why is this happening? the european council has come with its youth employment initiative. that eu strategy is a real failure; it was set up to support young people not in education, employment or training in regions with a youth unemployment rate in 2012 of above 25%. there are very large disparities between the youth employment rates in different regions. for example, in spain and cyprus the rates of youth unemployment in december 2013 were 54.3% and 40.8% respectively. in greece, the youth unemployment rate for november 2013 was 59%. in sweden it stands currently at around 25%. the problem is that most of the eu funding allocated to addressing youth unemployment will be allocated to countries with a much higher youth unemployment rate than sweden. so we are the backyard and we can pay for the other countries, which is totally unfair. as have i said already, solidarity is very easy when other people’s money is involved. youth unemployment is today again an item on the agenda of this house. again we have recommendations on how to tackle youth unemployment in the eu. again we are trying to make it easier for young people to move to jobs or training and to have eu support for quality standards for traineeships and apprenticeships. well, let me explain something: it does not matter how many times we put youth unemployment on the agenda, and it does not matter if we add another eur 300 billion from mr juncker to boost growth and jobs – the eu will not succeed. our taxpayers already contribute the role of the money to the eu and now again mr juncker wants to have more money for his first top priority as commission president. more money for what? more money to create jobs, which is pure communism and shows that mr juncker was running away on the communistic highway even before being elected. we want a better and stronger sweden, but above all we want our sovereignty back.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i think it is striking in this debate how weak the reasoning is on both the political left and the political right. the political right thinks that we will be stronger by closing our borders and making it impossible for the millions of jobs that are not created in the european union to be created. the political left, for its part, believes that the only way to create more jobs is through more money and public projects. the truth is, i think, rather, in a long list of other things that we have to look at going forward. how can we create common markets so that smes can function? how can we get a trade deal with the united states that will strengthen our economy? hardly any of my honourable colleagues have in any way raised the fact that we have just been through the deepest economic crisis in europe for almost 100 years. if we cannot create rules that create greater economies for businesses, we will not be able to afford to fix unemployment.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by expressing my support for the motion for a resolution on which parliament is to take a position today. one of the places mentioned in the resolution is the assyrian city of karakosh, where church bells rang for services half a millennium before christianity came to my country, sweden. however, the ringing of church bells in the city on june 25 of this year signaled something quite different from a service. now it was a desperate warning to the population, and just a day later the city was emptied of its population. the relatives and families who had lived in the city for generations were forced to leave everything behind - staying behind was not an option when the isis death squads advanced. in the neighboring city of mosul, church bells did not ring at all the following sunday. for the first time in 1,600 years, the city had no sunday services. madam president, reports of bloody suicide attacks on churches or christians being murdered by fanatical jihadists are increasing. in the face of such a situation, the outside world cannot stand idly by. in the short term, the advance of isis must be stopped and a government of national unity established. when a delegation of iraqi church leaders visited us here in the european parliament a week ago, they stressed the importance of support for the development of democracy and security. the eu and its member states must help. the situation requires us to help you across party lines. the resolution we have to adopt today is a step in the right direction, but condemnation in the european parliament is not enough. the whole of the european union, including the council and the high representative for foreign affairs, must now put this issue at the top of the agenda.
