in writing. - i've put my vote down. the proposed resolution highlights youth unemployment and the enormous problems it creates in the eu today. it stresses the importance of combating youth unemployment as an investment in the future. i agree with that. but the resolution does not single out austerity policies as the main cause of the extremely high youth unemployment. it should also emphasise more clearly the importance of an expansionary economic policy which, through green investment and increased prosperity, creates jobs and promotes gender equality and social justice. i also object to the demand for new supranational legislation in paragraph 3 and the demand for reduced taxes for companies in paragraph 43. that's why i've put my vote down.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in my group, we see the law, which has some flaws, as a good first step to improve the rights of children and youth in general in bolivia, not least because it has a rights-based focus and sees children as subjects rather than objects. the 348 articles include the prohibition of discrimination on grounds of sex or disability, the right to complain, the right of children and young people to a safe environment, education, information, personal privacy, etc. instead of threatening withdrawal of aid, we think the commission should be more involved financially, to actually support the implementation of the law and also to be able to contribute to children's schooling. is this something the commission could consider doing? someone has also mentioned this organisation for child labourers who have also been involved in this work of drafting the law.
in writing. i voted in favour of the eu-ukraine association agreement. however, i have several serious objections to the procedure itself, including the use of a so-called urgency procedure where the european parliament quickly votes through the agreement without being able to examine or discuss it. it is not responsible to represent the citizens in that way. the agreement is also subject to the approval by the ukrainian parliament through an expedited procedure, just weeks before the national elections in ukraine. it's not taking the democratic process seriously. a central part of the agreement is a deep and comprehensive free trade area (dcfta), with far-reaching implications for the economy as well as for social, environmental and food standards. the 'emergency procedure' used has prevented the competent parliamentary committee on international trade from examining the part of the agreement constituting the fta. unfortunately, the agreement has not been surrounded by a genuine political debate, as it should have been.
mr president, at a time when mr putin is probing our weakness and trying to redraw the borders of europe with military force, at a time when the people of ukraine are subject to an invasion with military force, i think it is very important that this parliament has united with a strong majority to show our support. i have voted in favour of the association agreement with ukraine wholeheartedly. it is very important economically, but it is also an important political signal that we do not accept this way of acting in europe. their problem, their fight for freedom, for sovereignty, for territorial integrity, is our common european fight. i do regret that both the left and extreme right have turned their back by voting against this agreement in a large majority . i think it is unacceptable; it goes against the european values we all stand for.
in writing. i voted in favour of the eu-ukraine agreement on the workers' rights part (the moraes report). the agreement stipulates that there must be no discrimination against third-country workers. it's important, you know. i would like to stress my criticism of the urgency procedure used, whereby the european parliament quickly votes through the agreement without being able to examine or discuss it. it is not responsible to represent the citizens in that way. the agreement is also subject to the approval by the ukrainian parliament through an expedited procedure, just weeks before the national elections in ukraine. the whole procedure is therefore a lightning-quick process that does not take the democratic process seriously. the agreement could have been improved if it had been dealt with by the ordinary procedure by both the european parliament and ukrainian parliamentarians.
mr president, i think we need to understand the logic of digital development. it is a worldwide development, which means that things are happening all the time: new products, new services, new generations coming up everywhere, here and there, and all the time. this means that, in this global market, you cannot be number two, because everyone will, in the long run, choose the number one. this means that if you are to be the leading digital single market, you need to be the most digitalised market. a more digitalised europe really means more europe. that is why we need to have structural reforms to get rid of 28 different local markets – it is so old-fashioned. we need to ensure that we can have lower costs and more investments, and that is why we need a single market with operators covering all the markets and delivering the best services. we need to aim for the highest capacities and the best speeds; because where you have those things, you will have new things developing. the services of tomorrow are not possible with the capacities of today. if we ensure that we have the best capacities, the highest speeds, the greatest spectrum, and the most opportunities, then we will see the new services of tomorrow and the day after that develop here in europe. the new products and the new companies, as well as our old companies, will be able to lead, and we will have the leading digital single market making the single market of the european union a reality. that is what we need to do. it is very simple, very complicated, but requires decisiveness.
madam president, we had a very unusual summer in sweden this year. we had the largest forest fire in modern swedish history. i do not know if you could even smell the smoke in denmark, but we actually had to call for fire-fighters from italy to help us. we had floods, we had sudden violent rainfall and the human and economic consequences of all this are enormous. when we look at the future, the consequences of a warming planet with competition for water and for farmland, with rising sea levels, we will see climate refugees and there will be enormous economic and human consequences. so how will we explain all this to our children and grandchildren? that the burning of brown coal was just so cheap that it was not profitable to switch to renewables? that we had the technology for renewables but we did not use it, we did not invest in it because it was not competitive? i would like to say to the council that we all know that the only way of making renewable energy and smart energy savings lucrative for the industry is if the eu sets binding targets that will drive global innovation and investment in this direction, so we can eventually leave the cheap and dirty coal in the ground. we know there is enough free and eternally renewable energy around for us to build a new clean society that we can be proud of and that is what i want to be able to tell my children and grandchildren that we did. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer. – thank you mr ferreira, it is clear that the emission trading scheme is not sufficient in order to replace fossil fuels and switch over to renewables, so we need the binding targets. i think that is the main political message that we can send to the industry and to research: that this is the future for europe. now to emit one tonne of co2 costs only eur 5 and to try to put it in the ground with carbon capture and storage costs eur 40; by that i mean it is too cheap to put this co2 in the air. that is a fact.
blue-card question. – i have a question for mr helmer. have you ever looked outside your own nation and your own parliament? because there actually are countries that are succeeding in having economic growth and strong industry and a strong economy, and still lowering their emissions from fossil fuels. in sweden we currently have more than 50% of renewables in our energy system. but, in order to achieve that, you need to have a government that is actually able to do it, and if industries are moving away from your country it is not because you have a strong scheme for saving the climate, it is probably because you have a too weak one that is not forcing your industries to be better at doing the right thing.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i want to address the prime ministers of europe who are now meeting to agree on the goals of climate policy. i want you to stand up for knowledge, science and rationality. do not give in to short-term interests and ignorance. you can negotiate down the targets and postpone the decisions. you can look away, but the problems don't go away. they're getting worse. they're affecting more people, and they're costing more. because climate change is real, and we all know the cause, and we all know why. it's about burning the fossil fuels, oil, coal and gas. but the solutions are already here. look at the countries that have invested in renewable fuels. they still have their industry. they have lower energy prices. i therefore appeal to you, the heads of government of europe, to stand up as adults. show that you are worthy leaders and take the responsibility necessary to meet the climate challenge.
mr president, four points that are important. first of all, a clear line versus russia and its war in ukraine. this is the most fundamental task for the european union: to preserve and defend peace. the aggression we see today from russia is a threat to the peace order of today. this needs the support of all member states and it must be clear and consistent. secondly, we need to attract investment into the european union. that means that we need to fight red tape, to use the new opportunities of having a vice-president of the commission responsible for better regulation and fighting bureaucracy. we can only attract investment if there is an opportunity for companies to profit, for companies to develop. we need capital markets that are liquid all over the european union. so we need to ensure that our regulation and legislation is not hindering liquidity-making and investment. thirdly, we need to secure credibility and the stability of public finances in the european union. how we face developments in those countries which are not living up to the stability pact will be crucial and decisive for the future. if we make new derogations for some countries just because they are big, we are making a big mistake. we did it in 2003; we must not do it now because it will damage the credibility of the whole of the european union. fourthly, the ambitions, the targets for reducing carbon dioxide emissions, are more important than the number of very many different targets. we need to focus and give priority to reducing carbon dioxide emissions, not try to steer the member states to where they can do things better. blue-card answer. – to be honest, i do not know on what planet you are living. we have seen for more than a decade that russia is a threat to european energy security, blocking energy distribution and using its energy resources for political blackmail. can you not see the fact that one country is invading another country in europe? if you are defending that, you are defending aggression. you are defending disrespect for sovereign countries, and you are defending those who are threatening europe’s peaceful order. you can do that because you are living in a free europe, but people like you are undermining our freedom. in writing. - the moderates in the european parliament voted today on the eu budget for 2015. as the budget was increased compared to last year, we were very sceptical that the european parliament wanted to increase the budget further, especially given that many eu countries suffer from deficits and high public debts. it is therefore important that the eu budget is used correctly and that politicians take responsibility for both sweden's and the eu's economy. for this reason, we have chosen to support the european parliament's demand for more funding for research, innovation and the completion of the internal market, as this is what brings added value to both sweden and the eu. in addition, it was important for us to support the eu's asylum and migration programmes to create safe and legal pathways into the eu, including by strengthening frontex. but we cannot accept that more of the taxpayers' money is spent on agricultural subsidies and huge subsidy schemes to support ageing and climate-damaging industries.
in writing. i voted against the budget. the budget is too large and too comprehensive. there should be major cuts. agriculture is just one example. it results in overproduction and negative effects on the environment, climate and livestock. another example is the uncontrollable regional and structural funds, where there is widespread fraud, as the commission itself has confirmed. i am opposed to the frontex initiative, which means the continuation of the construction of fortress europe, which forces people fleeing war and oppression to risk their lives to reach a place of refuge. this and the initiative to increase the militarization of the eu is the wrong way forward for a union which claims to defend human rights and is a peace project. i also think it is wrong in principle for the european parliament to have power over the budget. the member states that pay the eu budget should have full say on the union's expenditure. it is more democratic for the governments of the member states and the national parliaments to decide on the long-term budget. it is the taxpayers of the member states who pay and therefore it is their directly elected representatives in each country who should have the power. these are some of the reasons why i am voting against the budget.

for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. the council of ministers has now announced that the purpose of mos maiorum is to identify irregular migrants, i.e. undocumented persons in the eu, and that it is in this way that it wants to get at the criminal networks involved in human smuggling, who are taking huge sums for a place in a packed, seaworthy boat crossing the mediterranean from north africa to europe. this sounds very sweet, and of course these criminal networks need to be stopped! but hunting down undocumented people is a completely wrong approach to the problem. the result is that it is the undocumented refugee, or migrant, who is looking for work, rather than the criminal networks that are getting hurt. simply because the criminal networks and the traffickers are located and operate in the middle east and north africa, and are therefore outside the field of operations and the reach of mos maiorum. for many people, as mr sippel has said, the only desperate way for people to get to europe is through the smugglers, because there are no legal ways. instead of resorting to massive expulsions of undocumented people, we must now do more to strengthen the legal ways of entering the eu, as labour migrants, students, researchers, or workers. we must also take our responsibility for asylum seriously. if you eliminate the business of human trafficking and instead create legal and safe routes, then we will succeed. but then we need to harmonize and simplify the rules so that we can welcome workers from outside europe in a dignified way. as regards mos maiorum, i call for democratic transparency and for us to be able to check that fundamental rights are respected. there's a lot to say, but this operation should be terminated.

the questioner. - madam speaker, please. when the european parliament adopted the 2020 strategy, there were very high hopes. the crisis would be stopped, the financial markets would be regulated, jobs would be created and welfare would be increased. since then, however, things have gone in the opposite direction. today, some 25 million people in the eu are out of work. some six million of them are young people, who are therefore starting their adult lives in unemployment. in the wake of the crisis, social security and other welfare systems have been eroded, inequality has increased. the 2020 strategy wanted us to go in the opposite direction, but since 2008, the number of people at risk of poverty or social exclusion has increased by 10 million, so that today there are over 122 million people in the eu who are at risk of poverty or already poor and vulnerable. in our discussions today, but also in the light of the commission's proposal for an investment package, it is therefore important to stress that we must not forget to invest in what can bring people out of unemployment and poverty. we are of course in favour of investments in infrastructure, research and sustainable climate, but we must also invest in education and in increasing the participation of women in the labour market. that means building a functioning childcare system that is universal and accessible to all. we must invest in the integration and inclusion of those who are furthest from the labour market and, of course, we must do everything to ensure that no young person has to start his or her life as a long-term unemployed person. youth unemployment has reached alarming levels today. it is now over 20%, more than one in five young people in the eu, and we did not think so when we decided on the 2020 strategy in this house. in some cases we see more jobs being created and more coming in, but unfortunately the increases in the employment rate have been partly the result of the increase in very precarious and frivolous forms of employment, i.e. zero-hour contracts, false self-employment and involuntary part-time work. what was supposed to be flexibility has become a buoy of insecurity and exploitation. the fragmentation and abuse of labour law rules mean that people have never really managed to get a foothold in the labour market in recent years. of the many who do have jobs, over 40 percent are on temporary contracts, and as many as 25 percent are part-time. i meet some of these young people who are basically chained to their cell phones, because what if someone calls and offers them a few hours of work? then they can't miss that call. i mean, increasing economic growth is not in itself a guarantee of more quality jobs, of reduced poverty or social inequality. achieving these objectives requires the right policy choices. we must make the internal market work for people, so that it serves the lives and the societies in which we want to live. the benefits of growth must be spread throughout society to be sustainable. otherwise we are sitting on a ticking time bomb of inequality and human frustration with all the consequences it implies for both individual lives and our democratic society as a whole. we can never compete with our world by having low wages, poor education, poor uncertain working conditions, slave contracts, exploitation, weak trade union rights. the only way we can compete is to be the absolute best at what we do. it requires a great deal of thought and activity in this chamber.
madam president, in march 2000, eu heads of state and government agreed on an ambitious goal: making the eu the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-based economy in the world; the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-driven economy by 2010, capable of sustainable economic growth with more and better jobs and greater social cohesion – and where are we now? a huge crisis in the eu after this brilliant idea of the lisbon strategy. so what are we going to create now? of course, a new strategy! the eu is again promising a golden future, but after all these strategies we have the proof already: this house failed with these strategies, wasting money – wasting other people’s money, tax money. you do not even have the people in europe behind you. less than 43% voted in the last election. but as a socialist, of course, it is very easy to spend other people’s money.

in writing. - today we voted on financial aid from the so-called globalisation fund to people who have become unemployed after european companies have closed down. i am consistently voting against these unemployment benefits for two reasons: first, labour market policies and financial support for the unemployed should be decided on, and financed, at national level not at eu level. secondly, the fund's basic idea is that globalisation is something negative - which is not true. globalization and free trade lead to growth and peace.
in writing. - i voted for this report today. i am doing this in order to provide workers in these cases with financial support for retraining and/or other measures to find new jobs. however, i would like to stress that the widespread unemployment in the eu could be effectively combated if the austerity policies pursued for a long time by the european commission and the governments of the member states were stopped. to create new jobs in europe, a new economic policy with massive public and environmentally friendly investments is needed. investments for the benefit of society are needed in education, health and care, but also in infrastructure, railways, public transport, energy-efficient housing and sustainable energy systems.
in writing. we believe that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. we therefore oppose the european globalisation adjustment fund on principle, which we consider to be an expression of protectionism. we can perhaps accept the use of the fund as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, especially in view of the sense of hopelessness and exclusion that people feel in the wake of the crisis, and which risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, we believe that the worst crisis is now over and that the fund is therefore not justified in the not too distant future. to mark this, we chose to abstain in today's vote. the commission shall be empowered to adopt delegated acts in accordance with article 26 to amend the list of persons referred to in paragraph 1 by adding the following:

in writing. - today we voted on a resolution on the europe 2020 strategy. i voted against the resolution as a whole because it partly involves an increased burden on the eu budget by requiring increased appropriations for various labour market policy measures. in addition, there are articles on the european youth guarantee, minimum wages and flexibility in the labour market. these instruments are contrary to the principle of subsidiarity and contribute to greater control of member states' social and labour market policies. this is not a desirable development. the member states are responsible for deciding on social and labour market policy.
in writing. - we cannot support a resolution which calls for much of the same policies that led europe into the economic crisis from which it is now emerging. what europe needs is not more political governance and more public spending. the same policies have in recent years led to a weakening of france's competitiveness, rising unemployment and increasing deficits. there is no contradiction between jobs and growth. to create the conditions for jobs and growth, the eu must continue with ambitious structural reforms that make it easier to do business, stimulate research and development and make it profitable to work. labour market policy is a member state competence. the eu has an important role to play, not least through market reforms that facilitate free movement under social conditions and that contribute to trade and growth. labour market policy should not be managed from brussels. we cannot support the demands for a larger eu budget either. it is not eu contributions that create the conditions for jobs and growth in the member states, but increased investment in entrepreneurship and new jobs.

the questioner. - mr. speaker, i have to go. in a europe in crisis, security and justice have become exceptions. this is also very much the case in the labour market. it's definitely about the work environments that have become harsher and more dangerous. strong common rules on working conditions are a win-win for all of us. not just for those who work, not just for all citizens, but also for businesses. according to an international study, companies that invest €10 in preventive health and safety work can expect a return of more than double that amount. having strong common rules for a good working environment also means ensuring a level playing field. in order not to distort competition in the internal market, a common minimum standard is required. this, of course, must not prevent member states and companies from maintaining and demanding a higher standard. simplification of rules and reduction of bureaucracy must never be allowed to come at the expense of human life and health. proposals to exempt smes from health and safety requirements are not helping anyone, either the company or the worker. quite the opposite. large companies have the resources to cope with an accident or a long-term sick leave. in a smaller company, the margins are smaller, in a smaller company, it's the opposite: each individual employee means a great deal to you, and often you may have no insurance or staff to deal with if something happens. this is why osh issues are so important even in very small companies. everyone should have the right to the same good working environment, regardless of the company in which they work. that's my own conclusion. the proposals that have been tabled in this parliament have had a different starting point. they would exempt over 90% of all eu employees from complying with osh rules. such a proposal is, of course, totally unacceptable to many - i would say with certainty to a majority of members of this house. if these proposals are repeated in the next term of office, they will face very strong opposition. that is why it is so important that we address this issue at the beginning of this mandate. the solution is not to undermine the rules, but to make it easier for companies and individuals to comply with them. this requires an eu offensive programme. support systems are needed, new risk management tools for small businesses to facilitate osh work and strengthened trade union training that can make aware workers even better at handling difficult work environments and also contribute to real simplification real simplification, without worsening the working environment. the eu therefore urgently needs a new, aggressive osh strategy covering everything from workplace accidents to cancer risks and psychosocial injuries and stress. it's about thinking long term. that's radical thinking, you know? it's human to think. it is being a good union for working life and competition rules that allow us to remain strong producers of goods and services which we can then sell or use ourselves.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the convention on the rights of the child is one of the most important human rights documents in existence. today we have spoken a lot about the external dimension - what the eu can do for children's rights outside the eu - but the eu can never credibly put pressure on countries outside the union if we do not ourselves treat all children in our territory in accordance with the convention on the rights of the child. breaches of the convention occur daily in the eu member states. this is true of everything from refugee children who are locked up and denied the right to school and care, to various forms of physical violence such as child abuse, which is still permitted in a number of eu member states. in 2011, the commission adopted a communication on an eu agenda for the rights of the child. now is the time to take the next step: adopting a full-fledged children's rights strategy and accompanying action plan. it should cover all the eu's activities both within and outside the union. the well-being of children and young people shall be taken into account in all measures affecting them. this will also require coordination and resources. as the child is often forgotten when future issues are being considered, we social democrats proposed a commissioner with special responsibility for children's rights for the new term of office. but to no avail. this is an opportunity for the commission to reconsider its position. in addition, during the last term of office i asked the commission whether it planned to ratify the convention on the rights of the child on the part of the eu. that the eu would unilaterally commit itself to the convention. unfortunately, the response i received was subpar in the face of all the criticism. i hope the commission will resolve this.
madam president, in order to release all the jobs that we want to get from the new digital sector, in order to release all the inventions that might be possible from the new digital sector, we need to tear down barriers between 28 countries and consolidate one common legislation at european level. this is actually the big problem on the internet – not what one company is doing today or what it did yesterday. my big problem with this resolution is that a few parties think that they can fix the internet better than the internet is actually working by itself. because it is freedom that has provided us with the internet we have today; and it is freedom that is the nourishment of the internet. if we try to think that we can regulate – if there is an american-founded google, we should create one ourselves; if there is an american-founded facebook, we should found one ourselves; if there are cloud services in america, we should have them for ourselves – then we do not understand what the internet is. it does not care about any national boundaries whatsoever, nor does it care that there are national boundaries keeping development down, keeping the innovations down, keeping economy down and keeping jobs away. we have a great possibility here in this house, working with commissioner ansip, and also working with commissioner vestager when it comes to competition measures. because we are not the ones who can decide; we are not the body that is actually making the judgement. we are the body that puts the legislation forward and we do not make the judgements on different companies. just a few years ago we thought yahoo would prevail forever; we thought that was the system we would use for many years. today the market is totally different – a big shift in just a few years. i believe, colleagues, that if one of you invented a browser that was better than the ones we are using today, just in a few months the customers would change: the users would change and go to the best one, because we are also acting on a market and we do not pay for using the browsers. we do not pay for it, which makes the consumers and users very easy to transform. we can take example after example: a few years ago everyone had a hotmail e-mail address and then this was changed for a gmail one, and so on. this will keep changing the market. what the drafters of the resolution are saying is that they actually know better than the internet how it should be run and that they want the internet we have today to prevail. what we have to do is to create the internet of tomorrow, not copying the solutions that are already there but actually creating new solutions for the future.
blue-card question. – i actually agree with what you say on technical innovation – but do you? the epp group actually surprises me a bit on this resolution, because i think there is far too much market regulation and intervention. i usually understand when the s&d goes in that direction, but not when the epp goes that way. so, do you actually think that your regulation proposed on the table here is more important than working for net neutrality, where my colleague ms schaake today sent a letter with 131 signatures to the commission defending net neutrality?
mr president, i would like to thank you. a digital age needs a digital single market, but it is unfortunate that today we are dealing with this resolution instead of dealing with the actual issues that belong to itre and the juri committee, that is to say the legal affairs committee. some of the ideas put forward by the major parties here are quite populist, such as politically breaking down some of the major search engines on the internet into their constituent parts. it's pretty headless, actually. everyone understands that this is google. it is deeply inappropriate to engage in political finger-pointing in the commission's work on an ongoing competition case which is taking place in real time. i am grateful that we have a commissioner like mr ansip who knows what the digital single market really means and who knows what liberal ideas stand for in these contexts. in the area of the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights, the resolution has not caused as much confusion, which i am glad to see. we will continue to modernise european copyright, but we will also create a common copyright law and ensure that licensing is done properly. it is important that we build on the work done in the previous term, for example with the report on collective rights management and on-line music licensing for music. i think we should be putting more time and energy into this kind of concrete work instead of this kind of rather pointless debate in the plenary.
mr president, the digital single market can, if we do the right things, transform the european economy from being the world’s biggest economy to being the world’s biggest and most dynamic market, but that requires that we understand the freedom of the internal market and the reforms we need to make, but also the freedom of the internet, and that we are able to take decisive decisions that we more or less all know what it is about. first of all, we must refresh our targets for capacities and speeds. we cannot have outdated charges for speeds and capacities that belong to the past. we need to talk about gigabytes much more than megabytes in the future. we need to open up the 700 mhz band in order to ensure that we in europe can have the best preconditions for the new services. we need to ensure that we can develop pan-european network services with a high level of competition that can provide us with a single telecoms market without any roaming – not only without the cost but without the roaming as such. we need to ensure that we can have a vat system that is supportive of digital services. we need to ensure that we have freedom for users but also freedom for new service providers. we should not lack any of those, and the debate about net neutrality must in this sense support the emergence of new services. we need to ensure that we have competition at all levels and understand that if we do the right things, we will not need to regulate all the googles but can be the home base for the new googles, yahoos, blackberries and all the other apples that we can have, and also the ericssons, the nokias and the siemens. that must be our target.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when it comes down to it, nobody in this house is in favour of the eu, because when it comes down to it, everyone is acting in the best way to protect their own country and their own economy. look at france and germany, for example, with their huge car industries in peugeot, renault and volkswagen - they don't care much about eu laws, it's about protecting their own industry and economy. they're 100 percent backing their own industry and their own countries. i fully understand them, because if you are to compete with the big international companies, you have to take much better care of your own country's economy. companies in countries such as england, the netherlands and finland have been forced to close factories because of eu legislation. the commission has been forced to pay an unreasonable share of energy costs to finance renewable energy. i do not think that is fair. i'm not against renewable energy, but it has to be done in a fair way. the consequence of this is that production is preferred to other countries with a different cost structure. as a result, many workers lose their jobs. since 2007, 60,000 workers in the eu have lost their jobs to unfair competition. what will that number look like in the future if we continue like this? the protection of workers is not really a problem in this matter, but the fact that we have to protect workers is a consequence of the eu legislation in this house. it is quite clear to me that sweden and finland too, with their very important steel industry, must follow the example of france and germany with their car industry. the mining and steel industries have a long and traditional history in sweden. between 90 and 95 per cent of all steel production is exported and europe remains sweden's largest export market. the steel industry is very important for me, for us and for my own country to support in order to maintain employment and welfare in my own country, sweden.
mr president, i would like to thank you. pakistan is one of the most dangerous countries in the world for christians. for decades, the country's blasphemy laws have had devastating consequences for various religious minorities. one charge of blasphemy is enough to convict, to end a life. what happened in east pakistan on 7 november is a grim example of just that. a baseless accusation against the christian couple shama bibi and shahzad masih led to their being beaten by a giant mob before being burned to death. before we consider this resolution, the representatives of pakistan have assured me and other members that pakistan respects the rights of all its citizens. many of us doubt that assurance. therefore, the serious appeal to the pakistani authorities is: abolish the blasphemy laws. respect religious freedom. the abomination of november 7th must not be repeated.

madam president, i would like to thank you. unlike the vast majority of you in this room, i've spent most of my professional life in a truck cab. as late as march of this year, i parked the car after 30 years on the road. i have seen the problems that have arisen and i would say that the very biggest problems they have come from this house. when they introduced the cabotage legislation, that's when the cheating really started. because what is happening today is that unfortunately many of the big freight forwarders in europe - and we are talking about german schenker, german dhl, danish dsv - are using this cabotage legislation to carry out illegal domestic traffic. the third transport you are allowed to take in a seven-day period, you drive to it, from my country, sweden, to the neighboring country denmark and from denmark you drive to sweden, and then the car is back. we have over 2000 cars involved in illegal domestic traffic in sweden; this is proven by an investigation. it is not possible to say that this can be dealt with at national level, because it is under the umbrella of the eu that this is allowed to happen. it is eu law that makes this possible. you have to be strong here. the eu legislation is destroying the whole serious agricultural industry, because nobody wants to pay for what is needed for transport, because it is the last stage of a product and it should be as cheap as possible. it's at the expense of the drivers, and they're being used like slaves.
madam president, i thank the commissioner for a very good introduction. there are two points that must be highlighted. first of all, the road to undermined social security, increased unemployment, lost competitiveness as well as social unrest goes via spending money you do not have. if deficits were the solution, and if deficits could stimulate economic growth and employment, we would not have the crisis we are facing today. it is not consolidated budget policies that have caused the problems. the cause of the problems is the belief that you can solve economic problems by increased deficits and increased debt burdens. that is false, but that is exactly what we have seen. secondly, there is the review of the stability pacts. it might be good to do this, but even better would be to do a real review in those cases where the commission is dealing with deficit countries which have postponed their structural efforts and are not living up to the rules. credibility is about being credible. the european union must be credible; the commission must be credible; the stability pact must be credible. if we undermine their credibility by letting the big member states run away, not only from their commitments, but also from their rules and responsibilities, it will cost us all credibility. it will not help solve any problems, but it will create even more and make the crisis even deeper. be credible and sick to the rules. today, the european parliament voted on a joint resolution on the recognition of palestine as a state. i voted against the resolution and supported the epp resolution which was much more balanced than the joint text adopted by a majority of members. there are several reasons why i voted against the joint resolution. the european parliament has also called for the eu to take action to ensure that the palestinian people are not left behind.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the joint resolution on the recognition of the palestinian state as a principled step in the right direction, although some parts of the resolution are not in line with my views. recognition of the palestinian state, its people and its borders, and the end of the occupation is a long-overdue international obligation and a positive step towards peace. member states must now formally recognise and thus protect the space of the palestinian state on the 1967 borders with east jerusalem as the capital. palestinian recognition is established in un resolutions and a number of national parliaments of eu member states have called on their respective governments to recognise this. the palestinian people’s right to self-determination has been enshrined in international resolutions for 66 years. we call on all parties to refrain from any action which may undermine the viability and the prospects of the two-state solution.
in writing. - we have long been convinced supporters of a two-state solution where israel and a future palestinian state can live side by side in peace and security. such a lasting peace can only be achieved through negotiations between the parties and must be based on mutual recognition and respect for the agreements reached. this resolution adds nothing of value that could help to move these discussions forward because it is deliberately worded in a way that allows everyone to interpret the text in their own way. the only real result of this resolution is that we are adding to an already complicated situation a further ambiguity as to whether or not the european parliament has now recognised a palestinian state. for that reason, we cannot support the resolution.

in writing. - i fully support the main conclusion of the resolution to task the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs with conducting an assessment of the current policies of asylum and migration, including additional resources to perform this task. however, i abstained in the final vote because the text also calls for swift processing in collaboration with third countries of transit and origin and of return for those who do not qualify for asylum and protection in the eu. this effectively means that decisions of asylum claims are prejudiced, thereby seriously undermining the whole asylum system. processing of any claim for asylum should never be done in collaboration with the country of origin.

"absurd" is the word that best captures the way the lying cia directors dismiss the accusations. what has been discovered is a reminder of the impunity that exists for the many horrific human rights violations that the united states justifies in the name of national security. but as so many times before when the effects of torture have been investigated, the method has been shown to be pointless. but regardless of the security of the realm or the results achieved, i say it once again: torture is not acceptable under any circumstances, and the international prohibition of torture is absolute. it's true of everyone, everywhere. the report states that eu countries such as sweden, the uk, italy and romania have cooperated with the cia by handing over people to the us intelligence service without guarantees that they will not be tortured. and these countries, including sometimes my own home country of sweden, have let the cia fly people out in disgusting and degrading conditions. the time for denial must be over. the truth can set us free, and the truth must be told. impunity must end, the perpetrators must be held to account, the victims must be compensated and accountability demanded. blue-card answer. – i will be happy to answer. the brutality of the methods used and how they have been justified is simply unacceptable and the european member states must all come together to disclose the truth: what happened in your country as well as in my country. this is the truth and we need to see it now. it is time for impunity to come to an end, even in europe.
for the efdd group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. turkey is trampling on freedom of expression again. journalists are arrested and the media are constantly subjected to pressure and censorship. no country has been convicted more often in the european court of justice for violations of freedom of expression than turkey. it is a clear repressive policy, but it is wrapped up in beautiful cover and ringing words. despite pressure from the eu, turkey has not changed its mind, and i welcome the commission's protest against these systematic crimes against its citizens. some past events worth mentioning are the newspaper azadiya welat which has been closed down 8 times. former editor-in-chief of the newspaper vedat kurşun is serving a 166-year prison sentence and 9 journalists from the same newspaper are in prison. a few years ago, the reporter ersin çelik was sentenced to 6 years in prison, and at the same time, the journalist metin alataş from the aforementioned azadiya welat was found hanging from a tree. the eu is also pouring money into this kind of scandalous democracy. in 2013, over eur 900 million from the eu budget went to turkey. money that turkey received in return for a promise to improve, among other things, its broken rule of law, its non-existent freedom of expression and its rampant corruption. wasn't that money spent? it's clear to me: turkey will probably never be able to meet the copenhagen criteria.
mr president, first of all let me say that it is very simple: the turkish government must release all journalists and stop fighting free media. this is relevant regardless of what we say about turkey’s future in europe. this is about turkey being a member of the council of europe, but it is also about the requirement that all states in the world must respect free media, democracy and the rule of law. secondly, i think we need to do what we can in order to support developments which would turn turkey into a modern society with rule of law, an open society and a democracy with equal rights for men and women. it is that turkey of the future that we can discuss becoming a member of the european union. it has never been a point of discussion that the turkey of today should be a member. but for different reasons – maybe because some europeans have tried to close the door and maybe because some turkish politicians want to close the door – the problem is that mr erdoğan is moving his country in the wrong direction. not only away from possible membership but also away from being a solid, respectable open society. we need to call for a change in turkey. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer. – of course the issue about the divide in cyprus must be solved. that is part of what was happening in the negotiations and political agreements making it possible for turkey to have a european perspective and become a member. but let us also see the fact that the turkey that is orienting itself towards europe and doing the right things will also do the right thing regarding cyprus. a turkey that is turning away to russia or whatever will not do it. so i think we have a common interest in doing what we can in order to support a decent and good development in turkey, because that is good for europe.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in recent years, the georgian parliament has taken a position on a large number of proposals for new legislation and reforms to allow for deeper cooperation with the eu. the decision to approve the eu-georgia association agreement is the result of this endeavour and a historic step on georgia's road to freedom. georgia has chosen the european path. georgia has chosen this path despite russian threats, despite pressure. russia recognizes the importance of this agreement, and so does ukraine. earlier this year, ukrainian president petro poroshenko called the approval of his country's association agreement the most important moment in ukraine's history since the fall of the soviet union. the association agreement is a crucial issue for georgia's future. it is a crucial crossroads: should georgia be allowed to join the eu or should the georgian people be ruled by the men of power in moscow? we who vote for the association agreement are on the right side of history. those who choose to vote no are consciously choosing the path of vladimir putin. when the european parliament voted on the association agreement with ukraine, the efdd group, including its representative from my country sweden, chose to vote with putin. i think there are many of us in this chamber who are wondering what will happen this time? will the efdd group, or parts of it, continue to vote for putin or will it vote for georgia's right to shape its own future?
mr president, this is, as a lot of speakers before have told us, an historic step. it means that we are extending opportunities and a stable framework to the people in georgia and we are doing it to a country that is under an ongoing russian threat, with soldiers occupying parts of georgia. we will, of course, see renewed pressure after this, and it is important to state and clarify that any aggression towards georgia from russia is just as unacceptable as the moves we see from russia towards ukraine today. it is important also to see that this is an opportunity that must be used by georgia and the georgian government. it opens up free trade, investment and cooperation on a number of other levels and dimensions. this means that the georgian government must reform and proceed with the reforms in georgia. it means that the selective justice we see the georgian government using against its opposition today is not acceptable. at the same time as georgia is now moving closer to europe the georgian government must ensure that its society is moving closer to european democracy. it is about strengthening the citizens of georgia but also about utilising and benefiting from all the opportunities that this agreement offers. it is historic, but it is a challenge to the georgian government to stop selective justice, and it is important to say to russia: ʽstay out of georgiaʼ.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. more migrants than ever are coming to europe for the chance to make a life for themselves, simply. many are fleeing war and persecution, and because there are no legal and safe ways to get to europe to seek asylum here, most have no choice but to become dependent on utterly ruthless human traffickers to get on a boat and make a life-threatening journey to one of our countries. this year alone, 3,419 lives have been lost in the mediterranean, according to a unhcr report last week. the mediterranean is becoming a sea of death, a vast graveyard of nameless people united by only one thing: to save their own lives and the lives of their children and to seek a life of peace and freedom. now the presidency has initiated the khartoum process a cooperation between the eu and a number of regimes in the horn of africa, with the aim of preventing asylum seekers from going to countries like libya to make these dangerous journeys from there. political initiatives are needed, and i welcome this, of course, but no country can do this on its own, and i think it should never be imagined that regimes in the horn of africa that oppress and harm their people so badly that they have to flee will cooperate with the eu on this. i am mentioning sudan, somalia and the absolute dictatorship of eritrea, where the only prisoner of conscience in the eu, dawit isaak, is in prison today after more than 4000 days in prison without trial. are these the countries we're going to be working with? i question it, and i do it forcefully. i think we should look at this and the commission should define its own role in this better. the challenges are great, but we must never rely on dictatorships to find solutions to our migration problems. (the speaker agreed to answer a question (blue card) in accordance with rule 162 (8) of the rules of procedure).
blue-card answer. – your question is extremely important. what we need to do at this point is say that we will never accept dictators setting the rules for us as european free democracies, as the european union. we must keep in mind that it is, for example, one of our citizens, dawit isaak, who has been kept in prison for more than 4 000 days in eritrea, which is one of the harshest dictatorships in the world. are these the people that we are going to collaborate with? should we trust them to save human beings’ lives? let me tell you that frankly i doubt it. it is our responsibility, and we can never shrug it off on to those regimes in the countries in the khartoum process. i welcome the initiative, but i deem it to be a failure from the beginning, because we cannot speak with double tongues; we need to speak with one clear voice on freedom of speech, human rights, democracy and the rule of law.
mr president, i would like to thank you. yes, the eu has spent resources building fortress europe stone by stone, and now we are going to spend resources building a moat as well. this is completely unacceptable. moreover, aid money is to be used for this, and i would call that nothing but a waste. the overall objective is to control refugee flows, to return refugees and to condition cooperation. what if the starting point had been something else entirely, namely to increase the survival rate of all those people who are trying to escape from war and conflict? the initiative would have looked very different then. i am actually tired of my colleagues like frank engel and others in the right-wing groups who always, when it suits them, have another objective which is superior to human rights and the right to seek asylum. it may be that you want free trade, it may be that you want to control the flow of refugees, and then you can even deal with dictatorships about it. no, now is the time, not one more initiative that doesn't put people's right to seek asylum first.
in writing. i have voted against granting gsp+ status to the philippines. the trade agreement requires respect for human rights. on 4 december this year, amnesty international released the report above the law: torture in the philippines, which highlights a culture of impunity that allows the police in the philippines to continue torture unhindered. amnesty has launched a major campaign in the country to stop torture. in other words, it is clear that the philippines does not respect human rights. the eu must take a consistent line on these issues. in addition, the agreement includes a fisheries policy which involves a significant overexploitation of philippine fishing waters, as they do not comply with international regulations in this area. parties to trade agreements must respect their commitments both in terms of human rights and a sustainable and consistent fisheries policy.
in writing. i voted yes to the deal. i would like to stress, however, that the people of georgia should have had the final say on this important issue. the best form of a popular decision on crucial issues is a referendum; then the economic and social consequences of the agreement could also have been discussed in depth, as well as the consequences for relations with russia.
